Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A one-dimensional Japanese crossword can be represented as a binary string of length x. An encoding of this crossword is an array a of size n, where n is the number of segments formed completely of 1's, and ai is the length of i-th segment. No two segments touch or intersect.

For example: 

  * If x = 6 and the crossword is 111011, then its encoding is an array {3, 2}; 
  * If x = 8 and the crossword is 01101010, then its encoding is an array {2, 1, 1}; 
  * If x = 5 and the crossword is 11111, then its encoding is an array {5}; 
  * If x = 5 and the crossword is 00000, then its encoding is an empty array. 



Mishka wants to create a new one-dimensional Japanese crossword. He has already picked the length and the encoding for this crossword. And now he needs to check if there is exactly one crossword such that its length and encoding are equal to the length and encoding he picked. Help him to check it!

Input

The first line contains two integer numbers n and x (1 ≤ n ≤ 100000, 1 ≤ x ≤ 109) — the number of elements in the encoding and the length of the crossword Mishka picked.

The second line contains n integer numbers a1, a2, ..., an (1 ≤ ai ≤ 10000) — the encoding.

Output

Print YES if there exists exaclty one crossword with chosen length and encoding. Otherwise, print NO.

Examples

Input

2 4
1 3


Output

NO


Input

3 10
3 3 2


Output

YES


Input

2 10
1 3


Output

NO

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a one-dimensional Japanese crossword represented as a binary string of length `x`
2. The encoding is an array of segment lengths - each element `ai` represents a contiguous segment of 1's
3. No two segments touch or intersect (they must be separated by at least one 0)
4. We need to check if there exists exactly one crossword with the given length `x` and encoding array `a`

## Key Insights

For there to be exactly one crossword with given length and encoding:
1. The total length occupied by all segments must be exactly the sum of all `ai` values
2. The segments must fit within the total length `x`
3. The gaps between segments must be exactly one 0 (no extra spaces or missing spaces)

Let me think about what makes a valid arrangement:
- We have `n` segments of lengths `a1, a2, ..., an`
- Between each pair of adjacent segments, we need at least one 0 (to separate them)
- So we need at least `(n-1)` zeros for separation
- The total length used by segments is `sum(ai)`
- The total length used by gaps is at least `(n-1)`
- Therefore, the total length required is `sum(ai) + (n-1)`

For there to be exactly one valid arrangement:
- The total required length must equal the given length `x`
- If `sum(ai) + (n-1) > x`, then it's impossible (too many characters needed)
- If `sum(ai) + (n-1) < x`, then there are extra spaces that can be placed in the gaps (making multiple arrangements)
- If `sum(ai) + (n-1) = x`, then there are exactly one arrangement (all gaps are exactly one zero between segments)

Wait, let me reconsider the gap structure:
- If we have `n` segments, we need `(n-1)` gaps between them
- Each gap must contain at least one 0
- The total length used by segments is `sum(ai)`
- The total length used by gaps is at least `(n-1)`
- Total length = `sum(ai) + gaps_length`

For exactly one arrangement:
- We cannot have extra gaps (otherwise we could distribute the extra zeros in different ways)
- So the gaps must be exactly one 0 between each pair of segments
- Therefore, total length must be `sum(ai) + (n-1)`

But what about at the beginning and end?
- We could have leading zeros and trailing zeros, but if we add zeros at the beginning or end, we get multiple arrangements.
- For example: segments [3,3,2] with x=10
  - If we place segments with gaps of exactly 1 between them: positions 1-3, 5-7, 9-10 (wait, this doesn't work)

Let me reconsider with the examples:

Example 1: n=2, x=4, encoding=[1,3]
- Segments: 1 and 3
- Minimum required length: 1 + 3 + 1 (gap between) = 5
- But x=4, so impossible → output NO (matches expected)

Example 2: n=3, x=10, encoding=[3,3,2]
- Segments: 3,3,2
- Minimum required: 3+3+2 + 2 gaps = 10
- x=10, so exactly one arrangement → output YES

Example 3: n=2, x=10, encoding=[1,3]
- Segments: 1,3
- Minimum required: 1+3 + 1 = 5
- x=10, so we have 5 extra positions
- These extra positions must be distributed as zeros in the gaps
- We have exactly one gap (between segments), so we can place extra zeros there
- This creates multiple arrangements (e.g., gap could be of length 1,2,3,4,5)
- So there would be multiple crosswords → output NO (matches expected)

Therefore, the condition for exactly one crossword is:
- The total length must equal the sum of all segment lengths plus the minimum required gaps
- Minimum gaps = (n - 1) zeros between n segments
- So: sum(ai) + (n - 1) == x

Let me verify with examples:
- Ex1: n=2, x=4, a=[1,3]: sum=4, min_gap=1, total=5 ≠ 4 → NO ✓
- Ex2: n=3, x=10, a=[3,3,2]: sum=8, min_gap=2, total=10 = 10 → YES ✓  
- Ex3: n=2, x=10, a=[1,3]: sum=4, min_gap=1, total=5 ≠ 10 → NO ✓

Wait, in ex3 the calculation gives 5, but x=10, so 5≠10 → NO ✓

Actually, I made a mistake in ex3 calculation: 1+3 = 4, plus (2-1)=1, total=5, and x=10, so 5≠10 → NO ✓

But what about the case when there are no segments? (n=0)
- If n=0, then encoding is empty array
- Total length should be 0 + 0 = 0
- So we need x=0

But the constraints say n≥1, so we don't need to handle n=0.

## Algorithm

1. Read n, x and the array a
2. Calculate total_segment_length = sum(a)
3. Calculate minimum_gap_length = n - 1
4. Calculate required_total_length = total_segment_length + minimum_gap_length
5. If required_total_length == x, output "YES", otherwise "NO"

```python
n, x = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_segment_length = sum(a)
minimum_gap_length = n - 1
required_total_length = total_segment_length + minimum_gap_length

if required_total_length == x:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a one-dimensional Japanese crossword represented as a binary string of length `x`
2. The encoding is an array of segment lengths where each element represents a consecutive segment of 1's
3. We need to check if there exists exactly one crossword with the given length and encoding

## Key Insights

For there to be exactly one crossword with given length and encoding:
1. The total length of all segments must fit within the given length `x`
2. The segments must be separated by at least one 0 (since no two segments touch or intersect)
3. The total length used by segments plus the gaps between them must equal exactly `x`

Let me break down the calculation:
- Total length of segments = sum of all `a[i]`
- We need gaps between segments: there are `(n-1)` gaps between `n` segments
- Each gap must be at least 1 (since segments don't touch)
- So minimum total length required = sum(a[i]) + (n-1)

For there to be exactly one valid crossword:
- The minimum required length must equal the given length `x`
- If `x > sum(a[i]) + (n-1)`, then there are extra positions that could be filled with 0's, creating multiple possibilities
- If `x < sum(a[i]) + (n-1)`, then it's impossible (not enough space)
- If `x = sum(a[i]) + (n-1)`, then there's exactly one way to arrange the segments with exactly one 0 between each adjacent pair

Wait, let me reconsider the examples:

Example 1: n=2, x=4, encoding=[1,3]
- Sum of segments = 1 + 3 = 4
- Number of gaps = 2-1 = 1
- Minimum required length = 4 + 1 = 5
- But x = 4, which is less than 5 → impossible → should be NO ✓

Example 2: n=3, x=10, encoding=[3,3,2]
- Sum of segments = 3 + 3 + 2 = 8
- Number of gaps = 3-1 = 2
- Minimum required length = 8 + 2 = 10
- x = 10 → equal → exactly one solution → YES ✓

Example 3: n=2, x=10, encoding=[1,3]
- Sum of segments = 1 + 3 = 4
- Number of gaps = 1
- Minimum required length = 4 + 1 = 5
- x = 10 > 5 → we have extra 5 positions
- These extra positions could be distributed among the gaps (between segments) or at the ends
- Since gaps can be expanded, we have multiple ways to place the segments → should be NO ✓

So the rule is:
- If x > sum(a[i]) + (n-1): NO (multiple ways to distribute extra zeros)
- If x = sum(a[i]) + (n-1): YES (exactly one way - minimum gaps)
- If x < sum(a[i]) + (n-1): NO (impossible)

Therefore, the condition is: **x must equal sum of all segments + (n-1)**

But wait - what about the ends? Can we have zeros at the beginning or end?

Actually, the positions between segments must have at least one zero, but we can have zeros at the beginning or end as well. 

Let me think again: if we have extra zeros, we can place them either:
- At the beginning 
- At the end 
- Between segments (making gaps larger than 1)

Since there are multiple positions where we could place extra zeros, this creates multiple different crosswords. 

Therefore, if there are extra zeros (x > sum(a[i]) + (n-1)), we have multiple arrangements. 
If there are insufficient positions (x < sum(a[i]) + (n-1)), it's impossible.
Only when exactly x = sum(a[i]) + (n-1) do we have exactly one valid arrangement.

Let me verify with the examples:
- Example 1: n=2, x=4, a=[1,3] → sum = 4, gaps = 1 → min length = 5, x=4 → 4 < 5 → NO ✓
- Example 2: n=3, x=10, a=[3,3,2] → sum = 8, gaps = 2 → min length = 10, x=10 → 10 = 10 → YES ✓  
- Example 3: n=2, x=10, a=[1,3] → sum = 4, gaps = 1 → min length = 5, x=10 → 10 > 5 → NO ✓

Perfect!

## Solution Steps

1. Read n and x
2. Read the encoding array
3. Calculate total segment length = sum of all elements in encoding
4. Calculate minimum required length = total_segment_length + (n - 1)
5. Compare with x:
   - If x == minimum_required_length: print "YES"
   - Otherwise: print "NO"

```python
n, x = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_segments = sum(a)
min_required_length = total_segments + (n - 1)

if x == min_required_length:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a binary string of length `x` representing a one-dimensional Japanese crossword
2. The encoding is an array of segment lengths where each segment is a consecutive sequence of 1's
3. We need to check if there exists exactly one crossword with the given length and encoding

## Key Insights

For there to be exactly one crossword with given length and encoding:
1. The total length of all segments must be exactly `x` (or less, but we need to account for gaps)
2. All segments are separated by at least one 0 (since no two segments touch or intersect)
3. The total length of segments is `sum(a[i])`
4. The total length of gaps between segments is `(n - 1)` (since there are `n-1` gaps between `n` segments)
5. So the total length of the crossword is: `sum(a[i]) + (n - 1)`

For there to be exactly one such crossword, this total length must equal `x`.

Wait, let me reconsider with the examples:

Example 1: n=2, x=4, encoding=[1,3]
- Segment lengths: 1 + 3 = 4
- Gaps between segments: 1 (one gap between the two segments)
- Total length: 4 + 1 = 5 ≠ 4
- So it's impossible → NO (matches output)

Example 2: n=3, x=10, encoding=[3,3,2]
- Segment lengths: 3 + 3 + 2 = 8
- Gaps between segments: 2 (two gaps between 3 segments)
- Total length: 8 + 2 = 10 = x → YES (matches output)

Example 3: n=2, x=10, encoding=[1,3]
- Segment lengths: 1 + 3 = 4
- Gaps: 1
- Total length: 4 + 1 = 5 ≠ 10 → NO (matches output)

## Algorithm

1. Calculate the total length of all segments: `total_segments = sum(a[i] for all i)`
2. Calculate the number of gaps between segments: `gaps = n - 1`
3. Calculate the total length of the crossword: `total_length = total_segments + gaps`
4. Check if `total_length == x`

If yes, then there is exactly one such crossword configuration (the segments placed with exactly one 0 between each pair of consecutive segments).
If no, then either there's no valid configuration or multiple configurations exist.

Wait, is it possible to have multiple configurations when the total length doesn't match?

Actually, if the total length of segments plus gaps doesn't equal x, then it's impossible to have such a crossword at all. So there's exactly zero such crosswords.

But the problem asks: "Print YES if there exists exactly one crossword with chosen length and encoding. Otherwise, print NO."

So:
- If `total_segments + (n - 1) == x`: YES (exactly one crossword)
- Otherwise: NO (either zero or more than one, but since the length constraint is fixed, if it's impossible, there's zero, if it's possible, there might be more?)

Actually, let me think about whether there could be multiple configurations when the total length constraint is satisfied.

For a given encoding, once we fix the segment lengths and the constraint that segments don't touch, the positions are determined:
- We have `n` segments of given lengths
- We need exactly `n-1` gaps of at least 1 zero between them
- The total length of non-segment parts is `x - sum(a[i])`
- We need to distribute these `x - sum(a[i])` zeros among `n-1` gaps, with each gap getting at least 1 zero

If `x - sum(a[i]) = n - 1`, then we have exactly one way to distribute the zeros (each gap gets exactly 1 zero).
If `x - sum(a[i]) > n - 1`, then we can distribute the extra zeros in different ways (for example, give more zeros to one gap than another), so there would be multiple configurations.

So the condition for exactly one crossword is:
- `x - sum(a[i]) == n - 1`

This means:
- The total length of segments is `sum(a[i])`
- The total length of gaps is `x - sum(a[i])`
- We have exactly `n-1` gaps, so each gap must have exactly 1 zero.

Therefore, we need: `x - sum(a) = n - 1`

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: n=2, x=4, a=[1,3]
- sum(a) = 4
- x - sum(a) = 0
- n - 1 = 1
- 0 ≠ 1 → NO ✓

Example 2: n=3, x=10, a=[3,3,2]
- sum(a) = 8
- x - sum(a) = 2
- n - 1 = 2
- 2 = 2 → YES ✓

Example 3: n=2, x=10, a=[1,3]
- sum(a) = 4
- x - sum(a) = 6
- n - 1 = 1
- 6 ≠ 1 → NO ✓

Perfect! The solution is:

## Solution Steps
1. Read n and x
2. Read the encoding array a
3. Calculate total segment length = sum(a)
4. Calculate required gap length = x - total_segment_length
5. Calculate expected gap count = n - 1
6. If required gap length == expected gap count, print "YES", else print "NO"

```python
n, x = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_segments = sum(a)
required_gaps = x - total_segments
expected_gaps = n - 1

if required_gaps == expected_gaps:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a binary string of length `x` representing a one-dimensional Japanese crossword
2. The encoding is an array of segment lengths - each element `a[i]` represents a contiguous segment of 1's
3. We need to check if there is exactly one crossword with the given length and encoding

## Key Insights

For there to be exactly one crossword with given length and encoding, we need to consider:
1. The total length of all segments must fit within the total length `x`
2. There must be exactly enough space to place all segments with proper gaps between them
3. The gaps between consecutive segments must be exactly 1 (since segments don't touch or intersect)

Let me think about the structure:
- We have `n` segments with lengths `a1, a2, ..., an`
- Between each pair of consecutive segments, there must be at least one '0' to separate them
- So we need at least `(n-1)` zeros to separate `n` segments
- The total length occupied by segments is `sum(a[i])`
- The total length occupied by separators is at least `(n-1)`
- Therefore, the minimum required length is `sum(a[i]) + (n-1)`

For there to be exactly one valid crossword, the given length `x` must equal this minimum required length.

Wait, let me verify this with examples:

**Example 1:** n=2, x=4, encoding=[1,3]
- Segment lengths: 1+3 = 4
- Required separators: 1 (between 2 segments)
- Minimum required length: 4 + 1 = 5
- But x=4 < 5, so it's impossible → should output NO ✓

**Example 2:** n=3, x=10, encoding=[3,3,2]
- Segment lengths: 3+3+2 = 8
- Required separators: 2 (between 3 segments)
- Minimum required length: 8 + 2 = 10
- x=10 = 10, so exactly one crossword exists → YES ✓

**Example 3:** n=2, x=10, encoding=[1,3]
- Segment lengths: 1+3 = 4
- Required separators: 1
- Minimum required length: 4 + 1 = 5
- x=10 > 5, so there are multiple ways to place the segments? 

Wait, this is where I think I made a mistake. If we have more space than the minimum, we could potentially add extra zeros at the beginning, middle, or end. This would create multiple valid crosswords.

Actually, let me reconsider the problem. For there to be exactly one crossword, the length must be exactly what's needed by the encoding and the required gaps.

If we have extra length beyond the minimum, we can place zeros in different positions (beginning, between segments, or end), which would create multiple valid crosswords.

Therefore:
- The minimum required length is `sum(a[i]) + (n-1)` (segments + gaps between them)
- For there to be exactly one crossword, we need `x = sum(a[i]) + (n-1)`
- If `x > sum(a[i]) + (n-1)`, we have extra zeros that can be placed in different positions, creating multiple crosswords
- If `x < sum(a[i]) + (n-1)`, it's impossible (no space for segments or gaps)

So the condition is: `x == sum(a) + (n-1)`

Let me verify with examples:

**Example 1:** n=2, x=4, a=[1,3]
- sum(a) = 4, n-1 = 1, min_length = 5
- x=4 ≠ 5 → NO ✓

**Example 2:** n=3, x=10, a=[3,3,2]
- sum(a) = 8, n-1 = 2, min_length = 10
- x=10 = 10 → YES ✓

**Example 3:** n=2, x=10, a=[1,3]
- sum(a) = 4, n-1 = 1, min_length = 5
- x=10 ≠ 5 → NO ✓

Perfect! This matches all examples.

## Algorithm Steps

1. Read n and x
2. Read the encoding array a
3. Calculate total segment length: sum(a)
4. Calculate minimum required length: sum(a) + (n-1)
5. Check if x equals this minimum length
6. Print "YES" if equal, "NO" otherwise

```python
n, x = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_segments = sum(a)
min_required_length = total_segments + (n - 1)

if x == min_required_length:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to determine if there's exactly one crossword of length `x` with a given encoding `a` (array of segment lengths).

Key observations:
1. The encoding represents consecutive segments of 1's separated by at least one 0
2. Between any two segments, there must be at least one 0 to separate them
3. The total length of the crossword is the sum of all segment lengths plus the total gaps between segments

## Step-by-step Analysis

Let me think about what makes exactly one crossword possible:

1. **Total length calculation**: 
   - Sum of all segment lengths: `sum(a)`
   - Number of gaps between segments: `n - 1` (we need at least one gap between each consecutive pair)
   - So minimum total length = `sum(a) + (n - 1)`

2. **When is there exactly one crossword?**
   - If the given length `x` is exactly equal to the minimum required length, then there's exactly one crossword
   - If `x` is greater than the minimum required length, there are extra positions that must be filled with 0's
   - These extra positions can be distributed in various ways (between segments or at the ends), so there would be multiple crosswords

3. **When are there multiple crosswords?**
   - If `x > sum(a) + (n - 1)`, we have extra positions to place as 0's
   - The extra positions can go in different places: between segments or at the beginning/end
   - If we have extra positions, we can distribute them in multiple ways, so there are multiple valid crosswords

4. **When is there no valid crossword?**
   - If `x < sum(a) + (n - 1)`, then the length is insufficient
   - This is impossible since we need at least `sum(a)` 1's and `n-1` zeros to separate them

Let me verify with examples:

**Example 1**: n=2, x=4, a=[1,3]
- sum(a) = 4
- n-1 = 1
- minimum required length = 4 + 1 = 5
- x = 4 < 5 → impossible → should output NO (matches expected)

**Example 2**: n=3, x=10, a=[3,3,2]
- sum(a) = 8
- n-1 = 2
- minimum required length = 8 + 2 = 10
- x = 10 = 10 → exactly one crossword → YES (matches expected)

**Example 3**: n=2, x=10, a=[1,3]
- sum(a) = 4
- n-1 = 1
- minimum required length = 4 + 1 = 5
- x = 10 > 5 → extra 5 positions
- These extra 5 positions can be distributed among gaps (between segments or at ends), so multiple valid crosswords → NO (matches expected)

Wait, let me reconsider: can we have multiple distributions?

Actually, when we have extra positions, we can:
- Place some zeros at the beginning
- Place some zeros at the end  
- Place some zeros between segments

But can we distribute these extra zeros freely?

Yes - for example, if we have extra zeros, we can place them in different positions, which creates different binary strings. So if there's any extra space beyond what's required, there are multiple valid crosswords.

Therefore, the condition for exactly one crossword is:
- `x == sum(a) + (n - 1)`

If `x < sum(a) + (n - 1)`, it's impossible → but the problem doesn't say this case should be NO, it just says "exactly one".

Looking at the examples, when x is less than minimum, it would be impossible, so there's zero crosswords, which is not "exactly one".

So the answer is YES only when `x == sum(a) + (n - 1)`.

Wait, let me verify this with the examples:

Example 1: n=2, x=4, a=[1,3]
- sum(a) + (n-1) = 4 + 1 = 5
- 4 ≠ 5 → NO ✓

Example 2: n=3, x=10, a=[3,3,2]
- sum(a) + (n-1) = 8 + 2 = 10
- 10 = 10 → YES ✓

Example 3: n=2, x=10, a=[1,3]
- sum(a) + (n-1) = 4 + 1 = 5
- 10 ≠ 5 → NO ✓

Perfect! This matches all examples.

Edge case: what if x is less than sum(a) + (n-1)? That would mean we don't have enough space for the required segments plus the required gaps. This is impossible, so there are zero crosswords, not exactly one.

Therefore, the condition is simply:
**Output YES if and only if x == sum(a) + (n - 1)**

```python
n, x = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_segments_length = sum(a)
min_required_length = total_segments_length + (n - 1)

if x == min_required_length:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a binary string of length `x` representing a one-dimensional Japanese crossword
2. The encoding is an array of segment lengths (consecutive 1's)
3. We need to check if there exists exactly one crossword with the given length and encoding
4. Segments are separated by at least one 0 (no touching or intersecting)

## Key Insights

For there to be exactly one crossword with given length and encoding:

1. The total length of all segments must be less than or equal to the total length `x`
2. The segments must fit within the string, with gaps between them
3. The gaps between segments must be exactly 1 (since segments don't touch)
4. The total length calculation must account for:
   - Sum of all segment lengths
   - Gaps between segments (there are (n-1) gaps between n segments)
   - The gaps at the beginning and end (which can be 0 or more)

Wait, let me reconsider. The problem is asking if there exists exactly one crossword with the given length and encoding.

For there to be exactly one valid crossword:
- The total length of segments must be ≤ x
- The total "empty space" we need to fill with zeros must be exactly (x - sum of all segments)
- This empty space must be distributed in gaps: before first segment, between segments, and after last segment
- For there to be exactly one crossword, the empty space must be distributed in exactly one way
- But actually, we can distribute the empty space in multiple ways unless there are constraints

Let me think more carefully:

Actually, for a given encoding (segment lengths), there can be multiple valid crosswords unless the empty space between segments is completely constrained.

Wait - the key insight is: **for a given encoding, there can be exactly one valid crossword if and only if the total length of segments plus the gaps between segments equals exactly x, and the gaps between segments are fixed at exactly 1 (which is required since segments don't touch)**.

But what about gaps at the beginning and end? We can have any number of zeros at the beginning and end, and the gaps between segments must be at least 1.

For there to be exactly one crossword, the total length of segments plus the gaps between segments must equal x, and we have exactly one way to place all segments.

But the gaps between segments (between consecutive segments) must be at least 1 (since segments don't touch). So if we have n segments, we need at least (n-1) zeros for the gaps between them.

Therefore, the total length of the string must be at least: (sum of all segment lengths) + (n - 1)

And it can be at most: (sum of all segment lengths) + (n - 1) + (remaining zeros)

But the problem asks if there exists exactly one crossword.

Actually, I think the condition for exactly one crossword is when there's exactly enough space such that the gaps between segments are forced to be exactly 1, and the remaining zeros can only be placed at the beginning or end.

Wait, there's a mathematical way to think about this:

Total length x = (sum of all segment lengths) + (gaps)

Where gaps = gaps_before_first + gaps_between_segments + gaps_after_last

- gaps_between_segments ≥ 1 for each of the (n-1) internal gaps (since segments don't touch)
- gaps_before_first ≥ 0
- gaps_after_last ≥ 0

So minimum total length = sum(a_i) + (n - 1)

For there to be exactly one crossword, we need exactly one way to distribute the remaining zeros.

The remaining zeros after accounting for the minimum required gaps is: (x - sum(a_i) - (n - 1))

This remaining number of zeros can be distributed in (remaining_zeros + n) ways (by stars and bars - the remaining zeros can go to any of the n+1 positions: before first, between segments, after last, but between segments can't get more than what's left).

Wait, no - between segments we already have at least 1, so we can add extra zeros to between segments, before first, or after last.

So the number of ways to distribute r remaining zeros into (n+1) positions (before first, between first and second, ..., after last) is C(r + n, n) if r ≥ 0.

For there to be exactly one way, we need r = 0, because:
- If r > 0, there are C(r+n, n) ≥ 2 ways (since for r ≥ 1, C(r+n, n) ≥ 2 when n ≥ 1)
- If r = 0, there's exactly one way: all zeros are at the boundaries, but specifically: no extra zeros, so the segments are placed with exactly 1 zero between them.

Wait, let me check with examples:

Example 1: n=2, x=4, encoding=[1,3]
- sum = 1+3 = 4
- minimum required length = 4 + (2-1) = 5
- x=4 < 5, so impossible → NO (matches expected output)

Example 2: n=3, x=10, encoding=[3,3,2]
- sum = 3+3+2 = 8
- minimum required length = 8 + (3-1) = 10
- x=10, so remaining zeros = 10 - 8 - 2 = 0
- remaining zeros = 0 → exactly one way → YES (matches)

Example 3: n=2, x=10, encoding=[1,3]
- sum = 1+3 = 4
- minimum required length = 4 + (2-1) = 5
- remaining zeros = 10 - 4 - 1 = 5
- remaining zeros = 5 > 0, so multiple ways → NO (matches)

This seems to work!

So the algorithm:
1. Calculate sum of all segment lengths
2. Calculate minimum required length = sum + (n - 1)
3. If x < minimum required length, return NO
4. If x = minimum required length, return YES (exactly one way to place segments with exactly 1 zero between them)
5. If x > minimum required length, return NO (because there would be multiple ways to distribute the extra zeros)

Wait, is that correct? If x > minimum, we have extra zeros to distribute, so there would be multiple arrangements.

Yes! For exactly one crossword, we need exactly the minimum length - all gaps between segments must be exactly 1, and no extra zeros can be placed anywhere (since adding extra zeros anywhere would create different arrangements).

Therefore, the condition is:
- sum(a_i) + (n - 1) == x

If this equality holds, there's exactly one valid crossword (segments placed with exactly one zero between each consecutive pair, and no extra zeros).
If this doesn't hold, there's either no valid crossword (x < sum + n - 1) or multiple valid crosswords (x > sum + n - 1), so the answer is NO in both cases.

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: n=2, x=4, a=[1,3]
- sum = 4
- min length = 4 + (2-1) = 5
- 4 < 5 → NO ✓

Example 2: n=3, x=10, a=[3,3,2]
- sum = 8
- min length = 8 + (3-1) = 10
- 10 == 10 → YES ✓

Example 3: n=2, x=10, a=[1,3]
- sum = 4
- min length = 4 + (2-1) = 5
- 10 > 5 → NO ✓

Perfect!

## Implementation

```python
n, x = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_segments = sum(a)
min_required_length = total_segments + (n - 1)

if min_required_length == x:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

Wait, let me double-check edge cases.

What if n = 1?
- Then we have one segment of length a[0]
- Minimum required length = a[0] + (1-1) = a[0]
- So if x == a[0], we return YES
- If x > a[0], we return NO
- If x < a[0], we return NO

This makes sense - for one segment, we need exactly the segment length, and the rest of the positions must be zeros. But if x > a[0], we have extra zeros that could be placed at the beginning or end, giving multiple arrangements. If x < a[0], it's impossible.

For n = 1, with encoding [5], x = 5: should be YES
For n = 1, with encoding [5], x = 6: should be NO (multiple ways: 111110, 011111, 001111, etc. - actually many ways)
For n = 1, with encoding [5], x = 4: should be NO (impossible)

So the logic holds.

Final code:

```python

a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_segments = sum(a)
min_required_length = total_segments + (n - 1)

    print("YES")

    print("NO")
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a one-dimensional Japanese crossword represented as a binary string of length x
2. The encoding is an array of segment lengths - each element ai represents a contiguous segment of 1's
3. Segments are separated by at least one 0 (no touching)
4. We need to check if there exists exactly one crossword with the given length x and encoding

## Key Insights

For there to be exactly one crossword with given length x and encoding:
1. The total length of all segments must be less than or equal to x
2. The segments must be separated by at least one 0 between them
3. The remaining spaces (x - sum of all segment lengths) must be distributed in a way that creates exactly one valid arrangement

Let's think about the gaps:
- We have n segments of lengths a1, a2, ..., an
- Between consecutive segments, we need at least one 0 (to separate them)
- So we need at least (n-1) zeros for separation
- The total length used by segments is sum(ai)
- The total length used by zeros needed for separation is (n-1)
- So the total length needed is sum(ai) + (n-1)

For there to be exactly one crossword:
- The total length needed must equal x (if we can't add more zeros, there's only one arrangement)
- If the total length needed < x, we have extra zeros that can be placed in different positions (giving multiple arrangements)
- If the total length needed > x, it's impossible

Wait, let me reconsider. The extra zeros can only be placed in the "gaps" - before the first segment, between segments, or after the last segment.

Actually, for a unique arrangement:
- If there are minimum required zeros (n-1) for separation, and we have extra zeros, these extra zeros can be placed in different ways
- Specifically, we have (x - sum(ai) - (n-1)) extra zeros to place
- These extra zeros can be distributed in (n+1) positions: before first, between segments (n-1 gaps), after last
- If we have extra zeros (x - sum(ai) - (n-1) > 0), then we can distribute them in different ways, creating multiple arrangements
- Therefore, for exactly one arrangement, we must have no extra zeros: x = sum(ai) + (n-1)

Also, we need to check that the sum of all segments doesn't exceed x.

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: n=2, x=4, encoding=[1,3]
- sum(ai) = 1+3 = 4
- required length = 4 + (2-1) = 5
- 4 < 5, so impossible → answer should be NO (which matches)

Example 2: n=3, x=10, encoding=[3,3,2]
- sum(ai) = 3+3+2 = 8
- required length = 8 + (3-1) = 10
- 10 = 10, so exactly one arrangement → answer should be YES (which matches)

Example 3: n=2, x=10, encoding=[1,3]
- sum(ai) = 1+3 = 4
- required length = 4 + (2-1) = 5
- 10 > 5, so we have 5 extra zeros
- These 5 extra zeros can be placed in 3 positions: before first, between segments, after last
- Multiple distributions possible → should be NO (which matches)

Wait, what about the case where sum(ai) > x? That would be impossible.

So the conditions for exactly one crossword are:
1. sum(ai) <= x (otherwise impossible)
2. The total length needed (sum(ai) + (n-1)) equals x (otherwise there are extra zeros that can be placed in different positions)

Actually, if sum(ai) > x, it's impossible, so we need sum(ai) <= x.

And for exactly one arrangement, we need sum(ai) + (n-1) = x.

Let me verify with edge cases:
- If n=1 (only one segment): we need sum(ai) + (1-1) = sum(ai) = x. So we need the segment length to equal x.
- If n=0 (empty encoding): this would mean x=0, but x>=1 per constraints.

The constraints say n>=1, so we don't need to worry about n=0.

Let me check if sum(ai) + (n-1) = x is sufficient.

Yes, because:
- If sum(ai) + (n-1) < x, we have extra zeros (x - sum(ai) - (n-1)) > 0
- These extra zeros can be distributed in (n+1) possible gaps (before first, between segments, after last)
- Since x - sum(ai) - (n-1) > 0, we have at least one extra zero that can go in different positions
- Therefore, multiple arrangements exist

If sum(ai) + (n-1) > x, it's impossible since segments and minimum separators exceed length.

If sum(ai) + (n-1) = x, then we have exactly the required length with minimum separators, so exactly one arrangement.

Therefore, the condition is: sum(ai) + (n-1) == x

But we also need sum(ai) <= x, which is implied by sum(ai) + (n-1) == x since n >= 1.

Wait, if sum(ai) + (n-1) = x, then sum(ai) = x - (n-1) <= x, so it's automatically satisfied.

So the only condition we need is: sum(ai) + (n-1) == x

Let me verify with examples again:
- Example 1: n=2, x=4, a=[1,3] → sum=4, 4 + (2-1) = 5 ≠ 4 → NO ✓
- Example 2: n=3, x=10, a=[3,3,2] → sum=8, 8 + (3-1) = 10 = 10 → YES ✓  
- Example 3: n=2, x=10, a=[1,3] → sum=4, 4 + (2-1) = 5 ≠ 10 → NO ✓

Perfect!

## Solution

```python
n, x = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_segments = sum(a)
required_length = total_segments + (n - 1)

if required_length == x:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

The second line contains n integer numbers a1, a2, ..., an (1 ≤ ai ≤ 10000) — the encoding.

Output

Print YES if there exists exaclty one crossword with chosen length and encoding. Otherwise, print NO.

Examples

1 3


Output

NO

3 3 2


Output

YES

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a one-dimensional Japanese crossword represented as a binary string of length `x`
2. The encoding is an array of segment lengths - each element `ai` represents a consecutive segment of 1's
3. We need to check if there exists exactly one crossword with the given length `x` and encoding array `a`

## Key Insights

For there to be exactly one crossword with given length and encoding:
1. The total length of all segments must be less than or equal to `x`
2. The segments must be separated by at least one 0 (since no two segments touch or intersect)
3. The gaps between segments must be exactly determined

Let me think about the structure:
- If we have segments of lengths `a1, a2, ..., an`, then we need `(n-1)` gaps between them
- Each gap must contain at least one 0 (since segments don't touch)
- So we need at least `(n-1)` zeros for the gaps between segments
- The total length occupied by segments is `sum(a)` 
- The total length of zeros needed is at least `(n-1)`
- Therefore, the total length required is `sum(a) + (n-1)`

For there to be exactly one crossword:
- The required length must equal the given length `x`
- If `sum(a) + (n-1) == x`, then we have exactly enough space
- In this case, the zeros are distributed as: one zero between each adjacent pair of segments, and the remaining zeros (if any) can be placed anywhere in the gaps

Wait, let me reconsider. The problem says "exactly one" crossword.

Actually, if we have a fixed encoding and fixed total length, we need to check if there's exactly one way to place the segments.

For there to be exactly one valid arrangement:
1. The total length of segments must be `sum(a)`
2. We need at least `(n-1)` zeros to separate the segments (one between each pair)
3. The remaining zeros (if any) can be distributed freely in the gaps

But if there are extra zeros, then we could potentially place them in different positions, creating multiple valid arrangements.

Let me trace through the examples:

Example 1: n=2, x=4, encoding=[1,3]
- Total segment length: 1+3 = 4
- We need at least 1 zero between segments (since n=2)
- Total length needed: 4 + 1 = 5
- But x=4, so we don't have enough space (4 < 5)
- So no valid crossword exists → Answer should be NO

Example 2: n=3, x=10, encoding=[3,3,2]
- Total segment length: 3+3+2 = 8
- We need at least 2 zeros (between 3 segments)
- Total length needed: 8 + 2 = 10
- x=10, so exactly enough space
- The arrangement must be: 3 1 3 1 2
- Since we have exactly enough space, the zeros are fixed: one between each pair of segments
- So there's exactly one way to arrange it → YES

Example 3: n=2, x=10, encoding=[1,3]
- Total segment length: 1+3 = 4
- We need at least 1 zero between segments
- Total length needed: 4 + 1 = 5
- x=10 > 5, so we have extra 5 zeros
- These extra zeros can be distributed in the gaps (before first segment, between segments, after last segment)
- So there are multiple arrangements possible → NO

Wait, let me verify the third example. In example 3, n=2, x=10, encoding=[1,3].
- We have two segments: one of length 1, one of length 3
- We need gaps: before first, between, after last
- The segments must be non-overlapping and non-touching
- We can place the segments as:
  - 1 0 3 (length 5) - not enough
  - 1 0 0 3 (length 6)
  - 1 0 0 0 3 (length 7)
  - etc.
- Actually, we need to distribute the extra zeros (10 - 1 - 3 - 1 = 5 zeros) into 3 gaps (before, between, after)
- This is a stars and bars problem: number of ways to distribute 5 identical zeros into 3 gaps = C(5+3-1, 3-1) = C(7,2) = 21 ways
- So there are 21 different valid arrangements → not exactly one → NO

So the condition for exactly one crossword is:
- The total length of segments plus the minimum required zeros (n-1) must equal x
- That is: sum(a) + (n-1) == x

Let me verify with examples:
1. Example 1: sum(a)=1+3=4, n=2, so 4 + (2-1) = 5, but x=4 → 5 ≠ 4 → NO ✓
2. Example 2: sum(a)=3+3+2=8, n=3, so 8 + (3-1) = 10, x=10 → 10 = 10 → YES ✓  
3. Example 3: sum(a)=1+3=4, n=2, so 4 + (2-1) = 5, but x=10 → 5 ≠ 10 → NO ✓

Perfect!

## Algorithm

1. Read n and x
2. Read the encoding array a
3. Calculate total segment length: sum(a)
4. Calculate minimum required zeros: n - 1
5. Calculate required total length: sum(a) + (n - 1)
6. If required total length equals x, output "YES", otherwise output "NO"

```python
n, x = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_segments = sum(a)
min_zeros_required = n - 1
required_total_length = total_segments + min_zeros_required

if required_total_length == x:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

2 1

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of n integers and q queries
2. Two types of queries:
   - Type 1: Replace the i-th element with value x
   - Type 2: Replace every element in the array with value x
3. After each query, we need to output the sum of all elements

## Key Insights

1. We need to maintain the current sum of the array efficiently
2. For type 1 query: we need to update the sum by removing the old value at position i and adding the new value x
3. For type 2 query: we need to set all elements to x, so the new sum = n * x
4. We can maintain the current array and current sum, but we need to be careful about updating the sum efficiently

## Step-by-step Approach

1. Read the initial array and calculate the initial sum
2. For each query:
   - If type 1: update the element at position i-1 (since 1-indexed), and update the sum accordingly
   - If type 2: set all elements to x, and update the sum to n * x
3. Print the sum after each query

## Implementation Details

- Use 0-indexed arrays in Python
- For type 1: sum = sum - old_value + new_value
- For type 2: sum = n * x (and we need to update all elements to x)

Let me trace through the example:
- Initial: [1,2,3,4,5], sum = 15
- Query 1: t=1, i=1, x=5 → replace index 0 with 5 → [5,2,3,4,5], sum = 15 - 1 + 5 = 19 ✓
- Query 2: t=2, x=10 → all elements become 10 → [10,10,10,10,10], sum = 5 * 10 = 50 ✓
- Query 3: t=1, i=5, x=11 → replace index 4 with 11 → [10,10,10,10,11], sum = 50 - 10 + 11 = 51 ✓
- Query 4: t=1, i=4, x=1 → replace index 3 with 1 → [10,10,10,1,11], sum = 51 - 10 + 1 = 42 ✓
- Query 5: t=2, x=1 → all elements become 1 → [1,1,1,1,1], sum = 5 * 1 = 5 ✓

## Code Implementation

```python
n, q = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Calculate initial sum
current_sum = sum(a)

for _ in range(q):
    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if query[0] == 1:
        # Type 1 query: replace i-th element with x
        i = query[1] - 1  # Convert to 0-indexed
        x = query[2]
        # Update sum: remove old value at position i, add new value x
        current_sum = current_sum - a[i] + x
        a[i] = x
        
    else:
        # Type 2 query: replace each element with x
        x = query[1]
        # All elements become x, so sum is n * x
        current_sum = n * x
        # Update all elements in array to x
        a = [x] * n
    
    print(current_sum)
```

The solution has:
- Time complexity: O(q) - each query is processed in O(1) time (except the type 2 query which creates a new array)
- Space complexity: O(n) for storing the array

Actually, let me reconsider the type 2 query. Creating a new array of size n for each type 2 query would be inefficient. Let me optimize this. We can store the array and update it, but we can be smarter about the type 2 query.

Actually, in the type 2 query, we're setting all elements to x. The sum becomes n*x, and we need to update the array. Since we need the array for type 1 queries, we have to maintain it. Therefore, I'll implement the straightforward solution with list assignment, and hope that the test cases are not worst-case, or that in practice type 2 queries are not too frequent.

Alternatively, we can optimize type 2 queries by using a fresh array creation, but it's still O(n).

```python
n, q = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

current_sum = sum(a)

for _ in range(q):
    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if query[0] == 1:
        i = query[1] - 1
        x = query[2]
        current_sum = current_sum - a[i] + x
        a[i] = x
        
    else:
        x = query[1]
        current_sum = n * x
        # Update the entire array to x
        for j in range(n):
            a[j] = x
    
    print(current_sum)
```

This should work for the given examples and should handle the general case, even though it has O(q*n) worst-case complexity. In practice, it might pass if there aren't many type 2 queries or if the input is not worst-case.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of n integers and q queries
2. Two types of queries:
   - Type 1: Replace the i-th element with value x
   - Type 2: Replace every element in the array with value x
3. After each query, we need to output the sum of all elements in the array

## Key Insights

1. We need to efficiently maintain the sum of the array after each operation
2. For type 1 queries: we update one element and update the sum by adding (new_value - old_value)
3. For type 2 queries: we replace all elements with value x, so the new sum is n * x
4. We need to keep track of the current array state to know the old value when updating type 1 queries

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read the initial array and calculate the initial sum
2. For each query:
   - If type 1: update the array at position i-1 (0-indexed) with value x, update sum by (x - old_value)
   - If type 2: set all elements to x, set sum to n * x
3. Output the sum after each query

## Implementation

```python
n, q = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Calculate initial sum
current_sum = sum(a)

for _ in range(q):
    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if query[0] == 1:  # Type 1: replace i-th element with x
        i = query[1] - 1  # Convert to 0-indexed
        x = query[2]
        old_value = a[i]
        a[i] = x
        current_sum = current_sum - old_value + x
        
    else:  # Type 2: replace each element with x
        x = query[1]
        current_sum = n * x
        # Update all elements in array to x
        a = [x] * n
    
    print(current_sum)
```

## Trace through the example:

Initial: [1,2,3,4,5], sum = 15

Wait, the expected first output is 19, but my initial sum is 15. Let me recalculate: 1+2+3+4+5 = 15, but the example says after first query sum is 19.

Looking at the example: initial array [1,2,3,4,5], after first query (1 1 5): replace position 1 with 5 → [5,2,3,4,5], sum = 5+2+3+4+5 = 19.

So the initial sum should be 15, then after first query: 15 - 1 + 5 = 19. This matches.

After second query (2 10): all elements become 10, sum = 5 * 10 = 50. ✓

After third query (1 5 11): position 5 (0-indexed 4) becomes 11, so sum = 50 - 10 + 11 = 51. ✓

After fourth query (1 4 1): position 4 (0-indexed 3) becomes 1, so sum = 51 - 4 + 1 = 48. Wait, this doesn't match expected 42.

Let me recalculate the fourth query step:
- Before: [10,10,10,10,11], sum = 51
- After replacing position 4 (which is index 3) with 1: the element was 10, now it's 1
- New sum = 51 - 10 + 1 = 42. ✓

After fifth query (2 1): all elements become 1, sum = 5 * 1 = 5. ✓

The logic is correct. The issue in my manual calculation was using the wrong value - when we replace position 4 in [10,10,10,10,11], the position 4 corresponds to the 5th element which is 11, not 10. But the query says "replace 4th element" which is index 3, and the value at index 3 is 10.

So the calculation is correct.

```python
n, q = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Calculate initial sum
current_sum = sum(a)

for _ in range(q):
    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if query[0] == 1:  # Type 1: replace i-th element with x
        i = query[1] - 1  # Convert to 0-indexed
        x = query[2]
        old_value = a[i]
        a[i] = x
        current_sum = current_sum - old_value + x
        
    else:  # Type 2: replace each element with x
        x = query[1]
        current_sum = n * x
        # Update all elements in array to x
        a = [x] * n
    
    print(current_sum)
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Once upon a time Mike and Mike decided to come up with an outstanding problem for some stage of ROI (rare olympiad in informatics). Since then the first Mike has been waiting for an opportunity to propose the original idea for some other contest... Mike waited until this moment!

You are given an array $a$ of $n$ integers. You are also given $q$ queries of two types:

Replace $i$-th element in the array with integer $x$.

Replace each element in the array with integer $x$.

After performing each query you have to calculate the sum of all elements in the array.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two integers $n$ and $q$ ($1 \le n, q \le 2 \cdot 10^5$) — the number of elements in the array and the number of queries, respectively.

The second line contains $n$ integers $a_1, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \le a_i \le 10^9$) — elements of the array $a$.

Each of the following $q$ lines contains a description of the corresponding query. Description begins with integer $t$ ($t \in \{1, 2\}$) which denotes a type of the query:

If $t = 1$, then two integers $i$ and $x$ are following ($1 \le i \le n$, $1 \le x \le 10^9$) — position of replaced element and it's new value.

If $t = 2$, then integer $x$ is following ($1 \le x \le 10^9$) — new value of each element in the array.


-----Output-----

Print $q$ integers, each on a separate line. In the $i$-th line print the sum of all elements in the array after performing the first $i$ queries.


-----Examples-----

Input
5 5
1 2 3 4 5
1 1 5
2 10
1 5 11
1 4 1
2 1
Output
19
50
51
42
5

Initial array is $[1, 2, 3, 4, 5]$.

After performing the first query, array equals to $[5, 2, 3, 4, 5]$. The sum of all elements is $19$.

After performing the second query, array equals to $[10, 10, 10, 10, 10]$. The sum of all elements is $50$.

After performing the third query, array equals to $[10, 10, 10, 10, 11$]. The sum of all elements is $51$.

After performing the fourth query, array equals to $[10, 10, 10, 1, 11]$. The sum of all elements is $42$.

After performing the fifth query, array equals to $[1, 1, 1, 1, 1]$. The sum of all elements is $5$.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of n integers and q queries
2. Two types of queries:
   - Type 1: Replace the i-th element with value x
   - Type 2: Replace all elements with value x
3. After each query, we need to output the sum of all elements

## Key Insights

1. We need to maintain the current sum of the array efficiently
2. For type 1 queries: we update one element and adjust the sum by the difference between new value and old value
3. For type 2 queries: we replace all elements with the same value, so the new sum = n * x

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read the initial array and calculate the initial sum
2. For each query:
   - If type 1: update the element at position i-1 (0-indexed) and adjust the sum
   - If type 2: set all elements to x and update the sum to n * x
3. Output the sum after each query

## Implementation Details

- Use 0-indexed array for easier handling
- Keep track of current array and current sum
- For type 1 queries: sum += (x - old_value) where old_value is the current value at position i-1
- For type 2 queries: sum = n * x, and we don't need to update individual elements since we're replacing all

Let me trace through the example:
- Initial: [1,2,3,4,5], sum = 15
- Query 1 (t=1, i=1, x=5): change a[0] from 1 to 5, sum = 15 + (5-1) = 19 ✓
- Query 2 (t=2, x=10): all become 10, sum = 5 * 10 = 50 ✓  
- Query 3 (t=1, i=5, x=11): change a[4] from 10 to 11, sum = 50 + (11-10) = 51 ✓
- Query 4 (t=1, i=4, x=1): change a[3] from 10 to 1, sum = 51 + (1-10) = 42 ✓
- Query 5 (t=2, x=1): all become 1, sum = 5 * 1 = 5 ✓

This matches the expected output!

```python
n, q = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Calculate initial sum
total_sum = sum(a)

for _ in range(q):
    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if query[0] == 1:
        # Type 1 query: replace i-th element with x
        i = query[1] - 1  # Convert to 0-indexed
        x = query[2]
        # Update sum: remove old value, add new value
        old_value = a[i]
        total_sum = total_sum - old_value + x
        a[i] = x
        
    else:
        # Type 2 query: replace all elements with x
        x = query[1]
        total_sum = n * x
        # We don't need to update individual elements as we're replacing all anyway
        
    print(total_sum)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of n integers and q queries
2. Two types of queries:
   - Type 1: Replace the i-th element with value x
   - Type 2: Replace all elements with value x
3. After each query, we need to output the sum of all elements

## Key Observations

1. We need to efficiently maintain the sum of the array
2. For type 1 queries: we need to update one element and adjust the sum accordingly
3. For type 2 queries: we need to set all elements to x, so the sum becomes n * x
4. We need to be careful about the indexing - the problem uses 1-based indexing

## Algorithm Design

1. Read the initial array and calculate the initial sum
2. For each query:
   - If type 1: calculate the difference between new value and old value, update sum, and update the array element
   - If type 2: set the sum to n * x (since all elements become x)
3. Output the sum after each query

## Step-by-step Solution

Let me trace through the example:
- Initial array: [1,2,3,4,5], sum = 15
- Query 1 (type 1, i=1, x=5): Replace a[1] with 5. Old value was 1, new value is 5. Sum becomes 15 - 1 + 5 = 19. Array: [5,2,3,4,5]
- Query 2 (type 2, x=10): Replace all with 10. Sum becomes 5 * 10 = 50. Array: [10,10,10,10,10]
- Query 3 (type 1, i=5, x=11): Replace a[5] with 11. Old value was 10, new value is 11. Sum becomes 50 - 10 + 11 = 51. Array: [10,10,10,10,11]
- Query 4 (type 1, i=4, x=1): Replace a[4] with 1. Old value was 10, new value is 1. Sum becomes 51 - 10 + 1 = 42. Array: [10,10,10,1,11]
- Query 5 (type 2, x=1): Replace all with 1. Sum becomes 5 * 1 = 5. Array: [1,1,1,1,1]

## Implementation

```python
n, q = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Calculate initial sum
current_sum = sum(a)

for _ in range(q):
    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if query[0] == 1:
        # Type 1 query: replace i-th element with x
        i = query[1] - 1  # Convert to 0-based indexing
        x = query[2]
        # Update sum: subtract old value, add new value
        current_sum = current_sum - a[i] + x
        a[i] = x
        
    else:
        # Type 2 query: replace all elements with x
        x = query[1]
        # All elements become x, so sum is n * x
        current_sum = n * x
    
    print(current_sum)
```

The solution works as follows:
- We maintain the current sum of the array
- For type 1 queries, we update the sum by removing the old value at position i and adding the new value x
- For type 2 queries, we directly calculate the sum as n * x (since all elements become x)
- We use 0-based indexing for array access
- After each query, we print the current sum

Time complexity: O(q) - each query is processed in O(1) time
Space complexity: O(n) - for storing the array and the sum

This solution efficiently handles both types of queries and maintains the sum in constant time after each operation.

    print(current_sum)

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of n integers and q queries
2. Two types of queries:
   - Type 1: Replace the i-th element with value x
   - Type 2: Replace every element in the array with value x
3. After each query, we need to output the sum of all elements

## Key Insights

1. We need to maintain the current sum of the array efficiently
2. For type 1 queries: we need to update the sum by subtracting the old value at position i and adding the new value x
3. For type 2 queries: we need to update the sum by replacing all elements with x, so the new sum = n * x
4. We don't actually need to store the entire array if we only track the sum

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read n and q
2. Read the initial array and calculate initial sum
3. For each query:
   - If type 1: update the sum by removing old value and adding new value
   - If type 2: set sum = n * x (since all elements become x)
   - Print the current sum

## Edge Cases to Consider

- The array elements can be large (up to 10^9), so we need to handle large numbers
- The sum can become very large, but Python handles big integers naturally
- We need to track which elements have been updated when processing type 1 queries

Wait, there's a catch: when we get a type 2 query, we need to know the current values to update the sum. But we don't store individual elements, only the sum.

Actually, we don't need to store individual elements for type 1 queries if we keep track of the current values. However, for type 2 queries, we need to know what the current sum was before the update.

The key insight is that we don't actually need the individual array elements at all - we only need the sum. When we get a type 1 query, we need to know what the old value at position i was to update the sum correctly.

So we need to maintain:
- The current sum
- The current array (or at least the values at each position)

But storing the entire array is O(n) in space, which is acceptable since n ≤ 2×10^5.

However, we can optimize by not storing the entire array if we can deduce the old value. But for type 1 queries, we need to know the old value at position i.

Therefore, we must maintain the array values.

But let's think again: for type 2 queries, when we set all elements to x, we can simply calculate the new sum as n * x, and we don't need the previous values.

For type 1 queries, we need to know the old value at position i, so we must store the current values.

So we need:
- An array to store the current values
- A variable to store the current sum

Algorithm:
1. Initialize array `a` and `total_sum`
2. For each query:
   - If type 1: 
     - subtract a[i-1] from total_sum (since i is 1-indexed)
     - add x to total_sum
     - set a[i-1] = x
   - If type 2:
     - set total_sum = n * x
     - set all elements in array to x

Let me trace through the example:

- Query 1: t=1, i=1, x=5

  - sum becomes 50 - 10 + 11 = 51
  - array becomes [10,10,10,10,11]

  - old value at index 3 is 10

```python
n, q = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_sum = sum(a)

for _ in range(q):
    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if query[0] == 1:
        # Type 1 query: replace i-th element with x
        i = query[1] - 1  # Convert to 0-indexed
        x = query[2]
        # Update sum: remove old value, add new value
        total_sum = total_sum - a[i] + x
        a[i] = x

        x = query[1]
        total_sum = n * x

        # for subsequent type 1 queries

        # So we must update all elements
        for j in range(n):
            a[j] = x
    
    print(total_sum)
```

Wait, this won't work efficiently for type 2 queries because we're iterating through all n elements each time. With q up to 2×10^5, and each type 2 query taking O(n), worst case could be O(q * n) = 4×10^10 operations, which is too slow.

We need to optimize the type 2 queries.

Idea: Instead of updating each element in the array during type 2 queries, we can just maintain the sum and know that when we do a type 2 query, all elements become x, so sum = n * x.

But for type 1 queries, we still need to know what the old value at position i was. So we must store the individual values.

However, when we do a type 2 query, we're changing all values to x. So after a type 2 query, we know all elements are x. But we don't know which individual elements were changed in the past.

But actually, we don't need to update the array elements if we don't need them for type 1 queries. We only need them when we do a type 1 query.

For a type 1 query, we need the current value at position i. After a type 2 query, all values become x, so if we have a type 1 query after a type 2 query, we know the value at position i is x.

But if we don't update the array, then when we do a type 1 query, we would use the old value from a previous type 2 query, which might be wrong.

No - if we do a type 2 query, we set all elements to x. Then if we do a type 1 query later, we need the current value at that position, which should be x.

But if we don't update the array, the value might be old.

Actually, I think I made a mistake. Let me reconsider.

The array stores the current state. When we do a type 2 query, we need to update all positions to x. When we do a type 1 query, we need to know the current value at position i.

So we must either:
1. Update all elements in the array during type 2 query - O(n) per operation, might be too slow
2. Find a way to cache or track what values are current

But with 2×10^5 queries and potentially many type 2 queries, O(n) per type 2 query would be O(q*n) which is 4×10^10 in worst case - too slow.

Alternative approach: 
- For type 2 queries, we set the sum to n*x
- We don't actually update the individual elements in the array, because type 1 queries only occur when we want to modify a single element
- But when a type 1 query comes, we need to know what the current value at that position is

So we need to maintain the current value of each element.

But if we don't update them during type 2 queries, then when we do a type 1 query, we might get an old value.

Therefore, we must update the array during type 2 queries.

However, we can optimize the type 2 queries by noting that after a type 2 query, all elements are x. So we can set all elements to x at once.

But the problem is that q and n can be up to 2×10^5, so worst-case, if all queries are type 2, we do 2×10^5 * 2×10^5 = 4×10^10 operations, which is too slow in Python.

We need a better approach.

For type 2 queries, when we set all elements to x, we only need to update the sum to n*x.

But here, it's not specified.

Perhaps we can optimize by noting that if we do a type 2 query, we can just update the sum, and then for type 1 queries, we can use a different method.

But we still need the current value at position i.

Unless we maintain the array.

I think I have to conclude that the intended solution is to maintain the array and update it during type 2 queries, and hope that in practice, the number of type 2 queries is small, or the total work is acceptable.

But let me think again: if we do a type 2 query, we set all elements to x. Then if we do a type 1 query, we replace one element with a new value.

The array should be updated to reflect that.

So we must have the current values in the array.

Therefore, there's no way around it.

I will implement the straightforward solution. In many programming competitions, the worst-case might not be hit, or there might be additional constraints I'm missing.

```python
n, q = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_sum = sum(a)

for _ in range(q):
    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if query[0] == 1:
        # Type 1 query: replace i-th element with x
        i = query[1] - 1  # Convert to 0-indexed
        x = query[2]
        # Update sum: remove old value, add new value
        total_sum = total_sum - a[i] + x
        a[i] = x
    else:
        # Type 2 query: replace all elements with x
        x = query[1]
        total_sum = n * x
        # Update all elements in the array to x
        for j in range(n):
            a[j] = x
    
    print(total_sum)
```

This should work, and it's the most straightforward and correct solution. Even though it's O(n) per type 2 query, it's the only way to ensure correctness for type 1 queries.

If the test cases have many type 2 queries, it might be slow, but it's the correct approach for the problem as stated.

Let me double-check with the example:

Initial: [1,2,3,4,5], sum = 15
- Query 1: t=1, i=1, x=5
  - i=0, old value=1, sum = 15-1+5=19, a[0]=5 -> [5,2,3,4,5]
- Query 2: t=2, x=10
  - sum = 5*10 = 50, a becomes [10,10,10,10,10]
- Query 3: t=1, i=5, x=11
  - i=4, old value=10, sum = 50-10+11=51, a[4]=11 -> [10,10,10,10,11]
- Query 4: t=1, i=4, x=1
  - i=3, old value=10, sum = 51-10+1=42, a[3]=1 -> [10,10,10,1,11]
- Query 5: t=2, x=1
  - sum = 5*1 = 5, a becomes [1,1,1,1,1]

Output: 19, 50, 51, 42, 5 - matches!

So it's correct, even though it might be slow in worst-case.

I think for the given constraints and the fact that it's a programming problem, this might be the intended solution, or there might be a more advanced optimization I'm missing, but I can't see it.

You are given an array $a$ of $n$ integers. You are also given $q$ queries of two types:

Replace $i$-th element in the array with integer $x$.

Replace each element in the array with integer $x$.

After performing each query you have to calculate the sum of all elements in the array.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two integers $n$ and $q$ ($1 \le n, q \le 2 \cdot 10^5$) — the number of elements in the array and the number of queries, respectively.

The second line contains $n$ integers $a_1, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \le a_i \le 10^9$) — elements of the array $a$.

Each of the following $q$ lines contains a description of the corresponding query. Description begins with integer $t$ ($t \in \{1, 2\}$) which denotes a type of the query:

If $t = 1$, then two integers $i$ and $x$ are following ($1 \le i \le n$, $1 \le x \le 10^9$) — position of replaced element and it's new value.

If $t = 2$, then integer $x$ is following ($1 \le x \le 10^9$) — new value of each element in the array.


-----Output-----

Print $q$ integers, each on a separate line. In the $i$-th line print the sum of all elements in the array after performing the first $i$ queries.


-----Examples-----

Input
5 5
1 2 3 4 5
1 1 5
2 10
1 5 11
1 4 1
2 1
Output
19
50
51
42
5


-----Note-----

Consider array from the example and the result of performing each query:

Initial array is $[1, 2, 3, 4, 5]$.

After performing the first query, array equals to $[5, 2, 3, 4, 5]$. The sum of all elements is $19$.

After performing the second query, array equals to $[10, 10, 10, 10, 10]$. The sum of all elements is $50$.

After performing the third query, array equals to $[10, 10, 10, 10, 11$]. The sum of all elements is $51$.

After performing the fourth query, array equals to $[10, 10, 10, 1, 11]$. The sum of all elements is $42$.

After performing the fifth query, array equals to $[1, 1, 1, 1, 1]$. The sum of all elements is $5$.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of n integers and q queries
2. Two types of queries:
   - Type 1: Replace the i-th element with value x
   - Type 2: Replace all elements with value x
3. After each query, we need to output the sum of all elements in the array

## Key Insights

1. For each query, we need to update the array and calculate the sum
2. For type 1 queries: we need to update one element and adjust the sum accordingly
3. For type 2 queries: we need to replace all elements with the same value, so the sum becomes n * x
4. We can maintain the current sum and update it efficiently

## Algorithm Approach

Instead of maintaining the entire array (which would be O(n) for each update), we can maintain:
- The current sum of the array
- The current array (for direct access)

For type 1 query:
- Remove the old value at position i from sum
- Add the new value at position i to sum
- Update the array element

For type 2 query:
- The new sum is n * x (since all elements become x)
- Update the array (to all x)

However, since we need to maintain the actual array for type 1 queries, we'll keep the array and update it. The query operations will be O(1) for sum updates and O(1) for array updates.

Wait, let me reconsider - for type 2 queries, we don't actually need to store the entire array if we only care about the sum. But for type 1 queries, we need to know the current value at position i to update the sum.

So we need to maintain both:
- The current array
- The current sum

This gives us O(1) per query operations.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read n and q
2. Read the initial array and calculate initial sum
3. For each query:
   - If type 1: update element at position i-1 (convert to 0-index), subtract old value from sum, add new value to sum
   - If type 2: set all elements to x, sum becomes n * x
4. Output the sum after each query

## Implementation

```python
n, q = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Calculate initial sum
current_sum = sum(a)

for _ in range(q):
    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if query[0] == 1:
        # Type 1 query: replace i-th element with x
        i = query[1] - 1  # convert to 0-indexed
        x = query[2]
        
        # Update sum: remove old value, add new value
        current_sum = current_sum - a[i] + x
        
        # Update array element
        a[i] = x
        
    else:
        # Type 2 query: replace all elements with x
        x = query[1]
        
        # New sum is n * x
        current_sum = n * x
        
        # Update all elements in array
        a = [x] * n
    
    print(current_sum)
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of n integers and q queries
2. Two types of queries:
   - Type 1: Replace the i-th element with value x
   - Type 2: Replace all elements with value x
3. After each query, we need to output the sum of all elements

## Key Insights

1. For type 1 queries, we only need to update one element and adjust the sum accordingly
2. For type 2 queries, we need to set all elements to x, so the sum becomes n * x
3. We can maintain the current sum and update it efficiently without recalculating the entire sum each time

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read the initial array and calculate the initial sum
2. For each query:
   - If type 1: update the sum by subtracting the old value at position i and adding the new value
   - If type 2: set the sum to n * x (since all elements become x)
3. Print the sum after each query

## Implementation Details

- Convert 1-based indexing to 0-based indexing
- Keep track of the current array state (to get old values for type 1 queries)
- For type 2 queries, we don't need to update individual elements since we're setting all to x

Let me trace through the example:
- Initial: [1,2,3,4,5], sum = 15
- Query 1 (type 1, i=1, x=5): change position 1 (0-indexed) from 1 to 5
  - Old value: 1, new value: 5
  - Sum = 15 - 1 + 5 = 19 ✓
- Query 2 (type 2, x=10): all elements become 10
  - Sum = 5 * 10 = 50 ✓
- Query 3 (type 1, i=5, x=11): change position 5 (0-indexed) from 10 to 11
  - Old value: 10, new value: 11
  - Sum = 50 - 10 + 11 = 51 ✓
- Query 4 (type 1, i=4, x=1): change position 4 (0-indexed) from 10 to 1
  - Old value: 10, new value: 1
  - Sum = 51 - 10 + 1 = 42 ✓
- Query 5 (type 2, x=1): all elements become 1
  - Sum = 5 * 1 = 5 ✓

```python
n, q = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Calculate initial sum
current_sum = sum(a)

for _ in range(q):
    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if query[0] == 1:  # Type 1 query: replace i-th element with x
        i = query[1] - 1  # Convert to 0-based indexing
        x = query[2]
        # Update the sum: subtract old value, add new value
        current_sum = current_sum - a[i] + x
        a[i] = x  # Update the array
        
    else:  # Type 2 query: replace each element with x
        x = query[1]
        current_sum = n * x
    
    print(current_sum)
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given an array $a$ of $n$ integers. You are also given $q$ queries of two types:

Replace $i$-th element in the array with integer $x$.

Replace each element in the array with integer $x$.

After performing each query you have to calculate the sum of all elements in the array.


-----Input-----

The first line contains two integers $n$ and $q$ ($1 \le n, q \le 2 \cdot 10^5$) — the number of elements in the array and the number of queries, respectively.

The second line contains $n$ integers $a_1, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \le a_i \le 10^9$) — elements of the array $a$.

Each of the following $q$ lines contains a description of the corresponding query. Description begins with integer $t$ ($t \in \{1, 2\}$) which denotes a type of the query:

If $t = 1$, then two integers $i$ and $x$ are following ($1 \le i \le n$, $1 \le x \le 10^9$) — position of replaced element and it's new value.

If $t = 2$, then integer $x$ is following ($1 \le x \le 10^9$) — new value of each element in the array.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of n integers and q queries
2. Two types of queries:
   - Type 1: Replace the i-th element with value x
   - Type 2: Replace ALL elements with value x
3. After each query, we need to output the sum of all elements

## Key Insights

1. For type 1 queries: We need to update one element and recalculate the sum
2. For type 2 queries: We need to set all elements to x, so the sum becomes n * x
3. We need to efficiently keep track of the current sum and update it appropriately

## Approach

The straightforward approach would be:
- Maintain the current array and current sum
- For type 1 queries: subtract old value at position i, add new value x, update sum
- For type 2 queries: set sum to n * x

However, we need to be careful about the initial state and updates.

Let me trace through the example:
- Initial: [1,2,3,4,5], sum = 15
- Query 1 (type 1, i=1, x=5): replace a[0] with 5 → [5,2,3,4,5], sum = 5+2+3+4+5 = 19
- Query 2 (type 2, x=10): replace all with 10 → [10,10,10,10,10], sum = 50
- Query 3 (type 1, i=5, x=11): replace a[4] with 11 → [10,10,10,10,11], sum = 51
- Query 4 (type 1, i=4, x=1): replace a[3] with 1 → [10,10,10,1,11], sum = 42
- Query 5 (type 2, x=1): replace all with 1 → [1,1,1,1,1], sum = 5

This matches the expected output.

## Implementation Strategy

1. Read n, q and initial array
2. Calculate initial sum
3. For each query:
   - If type 1: update the specific element and adjust sum accordingly
   - If type 2: set sum to n * x (since all elements become x)
4. Output the sum after each query

```python
# Read input
n, q = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Calculate initial sum
current_sum = sum(a)

# Process queries
for _ in range(q):
    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if query[0] == 1:  # Type 1 query: replace i-th element with x
        i, x = query[1], query[2]
        # Convert to 0-indexed
        pos = i - 1
        # Update sum: remove old value, add new value
        current_sum = current_sum - a[pos] + x
        a[pos] = x
        
    else:  # Type 2 query: replace all elements with x
        x = query[1]
        # All elements become x, so sum is n * x
        current_sum = n * x
        # Update all elements in array (though we don't need to store them if we just use sum)
        # For correctness, we'll update the array as well
        a = [x] * n
    
    print(current_sum)
```

Wait, I notice that in the type 2 query, I'm creating a new array every time. This might be inefficient in terms of memory, but given the constraints (n, q ≤ 2×10^5), it might be acceptable. However, we could optimize by not actually storing the entire array if we only need the sum.

But looking at the problem, we need to handle type 1 queries which require knowing the current value at position i, so we do need to maintain the array state.

Actually, let's reconsider: For type 1 queries, we need to know the current value at position i to update the sum. For type 2 queries, we don't need the individual elements as long as we know the sum is n*x.

So we can maintain both the array and the sum. The type 2 query will make our array become all x's, so we can update the array to [x]*n.

The time complexity is O(q) which is acceptable for the constraints.

Let me verify with the example:
- Initial: [1,2,3,4,5], sum = 15
- Query 1: i=1, x=5 → pos=0, current_sum = 15 - 1 + 5 = 19 → array becomes [5,2,3,4,5] ✓
- Query 2: x=10 → current_sum = 5 * 10 = 50 → array becomes [10,10,10,10,10] ✓
- Query 3: i=5, x=11 → pos=4, current_sum = 50 - 5 + 11 = 56... wait, that's not right!

Oh no! There's a mistake. After query 2, the array is [10,10,10,10,10], so element at position 4 is 10, not 5.

So in query 3: i=5, x=11 → pos=4, current_sum = 50 - 10 + 11 = 51 ✓

In query 4: i=4, x=1 → pos=3, current_sum = 51 - 10 + 1 = 42 ✓

In query 5: x=1 → current_sum = 5 * 1 = 5 ✓

Perfect! The algorithm works.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given a string s, consisting of lowercase English letters, and the integer m.

One should choose some symbols from the given string so that any contiguous subsegment of length m has at least one selected symbol. Note that here we choose positions of symbols, not the symbols themselves.

Then one uses the chosen symbols to form a new string. All symbols from the chosen position should be used, but we are allowed to rearrange them in any order.

Formally, we choose a subsequence of indices 1 ≤ i1 < i2 < ... < it ≤ |s|. The selected sequence must meet the following condition: for every j such that 1 ≤ j ≤ |s| - m + 1, there must be at least one selected index that belongs to the segment [j, j + m - 1], i.e. there should exist a k from 1 to t, such that j ≤ ik ≤ j + m - 1.

Then we take any permutation p of the selected indices and form a new string sip1sip2... sipt.

Find the lexicographically smallest string, that can be obtained using this procedure.

Input

The first line of the input contains a single integer m (1 ≤ m ≤ 100 000).

The second line contains the string s consisting of lowercase English letters. It is guaranteed that this string is non-empty and its length doesn't exceed 100 000. It is also guaranteed that the number m doesn't exceed the length of the string s.

Output

Print the single line containing the lexicographically smallest string, that can be obtained using the procedure described above.

Examples

Input

3
cbabc


Output

a


Input

2
abcab


Output

aab


Input

3
bcabcbaccba


Output

aaabb

Note

In the first sample, one can choose the subsequence {3} and form a string "a".

In the second sample, one can choose the subsequence {1, 2, 4} (symbols on this positions are 'a', 'b' and 'a') and rearrange the chosen symbols to form a string "aab".

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.
