Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given a string s, consisting of lowercase English letters, and the integer m.

One should choose some symbols from the given string so that any contiguous subsegment of length m has at least one selected symbol. Note that here we choose positions of symbols, not the symbols themselves.

Then one uses the chosen symbols to form a new string. All symbols from the chosen position should be used, but we are allowed to rearrange them in any order.

Formally, we choose a subsequence of indices 1 ≤ i1 < i2 < ... < it ≤ |s|. The selected sequence must meet the following condition: for every j such that 1 ≤ j ≤ |s| - m + 1, there must be at least one selected index that belongs to the segment [j, j + m - 1], i.e. there should exist a k from 1 to t, such that j ≤ ik ≤ j + m - 1.

Then we take any permutation p of the selected indices and form a new string sip1sip2... sipt.

Find the lexicographically smallest string, that can be obtained using this procedure.

Input

The first line of the input contains a single integer m (1 ≤ m ≤ 100 000).

The second line contains the string s consisting of lowercase English letters. It is guaranteed that this string is non-empty and its length doesn't exceed 100 000. It is also guaranteed that the number m doesn't exceed the length of the string s.

Output

Print the single line containing the lexicographically smallest string, that can be obtained using the procedure described above.

Examples

Input

3
cbabc


Output

a


Input

2
abcab


Output

aab


Input

3
bcabcbaccba


Output

aaabb

Note

In the first sample, one can choose the subsequence {3} and form a string "a".

In the second sample, one can choose the subsequence {1, 2, 4} (symbols on this positions are 'a', 'b' and 'a') and rearrange the chosen symbols to form a string "aab".

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a string `s` and an integer `m`
2. We need to select positions such that every contiguous subsegment of length `m` contains at least one selected position
3. We then form a new string by taking the characters at the selected positions and rearranging them in any order
4. We want the lexicographically smallest possible result

## Key Insights

1. Since we can rearrange the selected characters in any order, to get the lexicographically smallest string, we should prioritize putting smaller characters first.

2. The constraint is that every window of length `m` must contain at least one selected position.

3. To minimize the lexicographical value, we should:
   - Try to use as many 'a' characters as possible (since 'a' is the smallest)
   - Then use 'b', then 'c', etc.
   - For each character, we want to use the minimum number of positions needed to satisfy the window coverage constraint.

4. The problem reduces to: what's the minimum set of positions we need to select such that every window of length `m` contains at least one selected position?

5. Actually, since we want the lexicographically smallest result, and we can rearrange freely, we just need to determine which characters we can get and in what quantities.

## Strategy

The key insight is that we need to cover all windows of length `m`. 

A greedy approach works:
- We want to use as many 'a' characters as possible
- But we need to ensure that every window of length `m` has at least one selected position

Let me think about this more systematically:

1. We need to place selected positions so that every contiguous subsegment of length `m` has at least one selected position.

2. This is equivalent to a "covering" problem where we want to cover all positions in the string with "windows" of length `m`.

3. The minimum number of positions needed is `ceil(n/m)` where n is the length of string, but that's not quite right either.

Actually, think about it: if we place a selected position at index i, it covers all windows that contain i, which means all windows from position `max(1, i-m+1)` to `min(n-m+1, i)`.

But a simpler observation: we want to minimize the overall string lexicographically, so we want as many 'a' as possible, then as many 'b' as possible, etc.

So the algorithm:
1. Find the minimum number of positions we need to select to cover all windows
2. For each window of length m, we need at least one selected position
3. Since we can place selected positions optimally, we can think of it as covering the string with intervals of length m

Wait, let me reconsider with the examples:

Example 1: m=3, s="cbabc"
- Windows: [0,2]="cba", [1,3]="bab", [2,4]="abc"
- We need to cover all 3 windows
- If we select position 2 (0-indexed), it covers windows [1,3] and [2,4]? Let me check:
  - Position 2 covers window starting at 0? Only if 0 ≤ 2 ≤ 2 → yes (covers [0,2])
  - Position 2 covers window starting at 1? 1 ≤ 2 ≤ 3 → yes (covers [1,3])
  - Position 2 covers window starting at 2? 2 ≤ 2 ≤ 4 → yes (covers [2,4])
- So one position at index 2 is enough
- Character at index 2 is 'a', so answer is "a"

Example 2: m=2, s="abcab"
- Windows: [0,1]="ab", [1,2]="bc", [2,3]="ca", [3,4]="ab"
- We need to cover all 4 windows
- Can we do it with just 'a' characters?
  - Position 0: covers windows [0,1]
  - Position 3: covers windows [2,3] and [3,4]? Window [2,3] → contains position 3, yes. Window [3,4] → contains position 3, yes.
  - But window [1,2]="bc" - is it covered? Position 0 covers [0,1], position 3 covers [2,3] and [3,4]. Window [1,2] starts at 1, ends at 2. Is there a selected position in [1,2]? Only if position 1 or 2 is selected.
  - So we need at least one position in window [1,2]
- If we select positions 0, 3, and 4: indices 0,3,4
  - 0 covers [0,1]
  - 3 covers [2,3] and [3,4] 
  - 4 would cover [3,4] but not [1,2]
- What if we select positions 1, 3? 
  - Position 1 covers [0,1] and [1,2]? Window [0,1]: 0≤1≤1 → yes. Window [1,2]: 1≤1≤2 → yes. Window [2,3]: 2≤1≤3? no. So not covered.
- What if we select positions 0, 2, 4?
  - 0: covers [0,1]
  - 2: covers [1,2] and [2,3]? Window [1,2]: 1≤2≤2 → yes. Window [2,3]: 2≤2≤3 → yes. Window [3,4]: 3≤2≤4? no.
- What if we select positions 0, 1, 3?
  - 0: covers [0,1]
  - 1: covers [0,1] and [1,2] 
  - 3: covers [2,3] and [3,4]
  - All windows covered.
  - Characters: s[0]='a', s[1]='b', s[3]='a' → we can rearrange to "aab"

So the constraint is that every window must have at least one selected position.

## Optimal Strategy

To get lexicographically smallest result, we should:
1. Try to use as many 'a' characters as possible
2. Only when forced, use 'b', then 'c', etc.

The key insight is that we can use a greedy covering algorithm where we go through the string from left to right and place selected positions as needed.

But we want to minimize the lexicographical value, so we want to prioritize using 'a' characters.

Actually, here's a better approach:
- We need to cover all windows of length m
- The positions that cover a window starting at i are the positions in [i, i+m-1]
- We want to select a set of positions that covers all windows
- Among all such covering sets, we want the one that gives lexicographically smallest string when sorted

But since we can rearrange arbitrarily, the lexicographic order of the resulting string only depends on the multiset of characters at the selected positions.

So we want to minimize the multiset of selected characters in lexicographical order.

To do this, we can:
1. For each position i, if we select it, we get s[i]
2. We need to select a set that covers all windows
3. We want to minimize the resulting string when sorted

This is equivalent to: find the minimum multiset of characters such that the selected positions cover all windows of length m.

Since we can choose any subset that satisfies the coverage constraint, and we want lexicographically smallest result, we should:
- Try to use as many 'a' as possible
- Then as many 'b' as possible, etc.

But how to find the minimum number and distribution?

I think a greedy covering algorithm works:
- Start from leftmost window (starting at 0)
- Find the rightmost position that can cover this window (i.e. position m-1) - this minimizes the "spread" of future coverage
- Or find the leftmost position that can cover this window? 

Actually, to minimize the number of remaining windows, we should place a selected position as far to the right as possible within the current window. This way, it will cover more subsequent windows.

But we want lexicographically smallest string, not minimum number of selections.

Since all selections of the same character will give the same when sorted, we want to:
1. First see how many 'a' we can use
2. Then how many 'b', etc.

But can we use all available 'a' in positions that cover windows?

Actually, no - we have to satisfy the covering constraint.

Here's a different approach:
- The minimum number of selections needed is `ceil(n/m)` where n is the length of string, but that's not correct for m=2, n=5: ceil(5/2)=3.
- For m=2, n=5, windows: [0,1],[1,2],[2,3],[3,4] → we need at least 3 selections? Yes, because each selection covers at most 2 windows, and we have 4 windows.

But actually, a position at index i covers windows that start from max(0, i-m+1) to min(n-m, i).

Wait, window starting at j covers positions from j to j+m-1.

So a position at index i covers all windows j where j ≤ i ≤ j+m-1, which means j ≥ i-m+1 and j ≤ i.

So the position i covers windows from max(0, i-m+1) to min(n-m, i).

To cover all windows, we need to place selected positions such that the union of these intervals covers all windows.

This is a classic interval covering problem.

But since we want lexicographically smallest result, and we can choose which positions to select, we can use a greedy algorithm that repeatedly places a selection to cover the leftmost uncovered window.

The greedy algorithm for covering intervals:
- Go from left to right, find the leftmost uncovered window
- Place a selection at the rightmost position in that window (i.e., at position i+m-1) to cover as many future windows as possible
- Remove all windows that are now covered by this selection

This minimizes the total number of selections needed.

But does this give lexicographically smallest character multiset?

Not necessarily, because we might prefer to use a position with 'a' over a position with 'b' if they cover the same uncovered window.

For example, if a window [i,i+m-1] contains both 'a' and 'b', and we have a choice, we should prefer 'a'.

So modified greedy:
- Go from left to right, find the first uncovered window
- Among all positions in that window, choose the one that has the smallest character, and place a selection there
- This will give us the lexicographically smallest character when we sort

But is this safe? Could choosing a 'a' position early prevent us from using a better 'a' later?

Actually, since we're going left to right and covering windows, and we want to minimize the lexicographic value, choosing the smallest character in the current window is optimal for the immediate goal.

However, we also need to consider that placing a selection at a position might cover more future windows.

But in this problem, when we place a selection, we're only interested in the final multiset of characters, not the number of selections.

Since we can rearrange arbitrarily, the lexicographic order of the final string is determined entirely by the multiset of characters we select.

Therefore, to minimize the lexicographic result, we should try to use as many small characters as possible.

The optimal strategy is:
1. Use a greedy covering algorithm that covers windows from left to right
2. When we need to cover a new window, we choose the position within that window that has the smallest character, among all positions that would cover this window (i.e., in the range [start, start+m-1])

But wait, when we place a selection at position i, it covers all windows j such that j ≤ i ≤ j+m-1.

So when we're covering a window starting at j, we can choose any position i in [j, j+m-1] to place our selection, and this selection will cover windows from max(0, i-m+1) to min(n-m, i).

To cover window j, we need to select a position i in [j, j+m-1].

The greedy choice: for the leftmost uncovered window (say starting at position j), we choose the position i in [j, j+m-1] that has the smallest character s[i], and place our selection there.

This ensures that we use the lexicographically smallest character possible at each step.

Let me trace through examples:

Example 1: m=3, s="cbabc"
- Windows: start at 0,1,2
- Start with window 0: positions in [0,2] are c,b,a → min character is 'a' at index 2

- Selected positions: 0,1,3 → characters: s[0]='a', s[1]='b', s[3]='a' → multiset: {'a','a','b'} → sorted: "aab" → correct!

- Windows: start at 0,1,2,3,4,5,6
- n=11, so windows start from 0 to 8 (since 11-3+1=9? Wait: 11-3+1=9, so 9 windows? Let me count: indices 0 to 8)

Wait, "bcabcbaccba" has 11 characters, so windows start from 0 to 8 (inclusive), that's 9 windows.

- This covers windows j where j ≤ 3 ≤ j+2 → j ≥ 1 and j ≤ 3 → covers windows 1,2,3

- Actually: j ≤ 3 ≤ j+2, so j ≥ 1 and j ≤ 3 → j=1,2,3
- So window 3 is covered

- Choose any, say index 4 (character 'b')

- Positions for window 8: [8,10]: s[8]='c', s[9]='b', s[10]='a' → min is 'a' at index 10
- Place selection at index 10 (character 'a')
- This covers windows j where j ≤ 10 ≤ j+2 → j ≥ 8 and j ≤ 10 → covers windows 8,9? Wait, windows go from 0 to 8 (length 9)

- So covers window 8

All windows covered.
Selected characters: 
- index 2: 'a'
- index 3: 'a'  
- index 4: 'b'
- index 7: 'a'
- index 10: 'a'

Multiset: a,a,a,b,a → five 'a's and one 'b' → sorted: "aaabb" → matches expected output!

Perfect!

## Algorithm

1. Convert string to list of characters for easy access
2. Use a set to track which windows are covered
3. Iterate from window start = 0 to n-m (inclusive)
4. For each uncovered window, find the position in [start, start+m-1] that has the smallest character
5. Place a selection at that position, and mark all windows that this position covers as covered
6. Add the character at that position to our result list
7. Sort the result list to get the lexicographically smallest string

But we don't need to sort at the end - we are building a multiset of characters, and since we can rearrange freely, we just need to collect the characters.

Actually, we just need to collect all selected characters, then sort them.

So steps:
- Initialize covered set as empty (for windows)
- Initialize result list as empty
- For start from 0 to n-m:
  - If window start is not covered:
    - Find min_char and its position in [start, start+m-1]
    - Add s[position] to result
    - Mark all windows j such that j ≤ position ≤ j+m-1 as covered (i.e., j such that j ≥ max(0, position-m+1) and j ≤ min(n-m, position) )
- Sort result and join

But we need to be careful about the coverage range.

For a selection at position pos, it covers windows j where:
- j ≤ pos ≤ j + m - 1
- So j ≥ pos - m + 1 and j ≤ pos
- Also j ≥ 0 and j ≤ n - m

So j ranges from max(0, pos - m + 1) to min(n - m, pos)

We can use a boolean array or a set to track covered windows.

Since n ≤ 100,000, and number of windows is at most 100,000, this is acceptable.

Let me implement this.

```python
m = int(input())
s = input().strip()
n = len(s)

# covered[j] means window starting at j is covered
covered = [False] * (n - m + 1)  # windows from 0 to n-m
result_chars = []

# Iterate through each window
for start in range(n - m + 1):
    if covered[start]:
        continue
    
    # Find the position in [start, start+m-1] that has minimum character
    min_char = s[start]
    min_pos = start
    for pos in range(start, start + m):
        if s[pos] < min_char:
            min_char = s[pos]
            min_pos = pos
    
    # Add this character to result
    result_chars.append(s[min_pos])
    
    # Mark all windows that are covered by this selection
    # A window starting at j is covered if j <= min_pos <= j + m - 1
    # i.e., j >= min_pos - m + 1 and j <= min_pos
    # and j in [0, n-m]
    j_min = max(0, min_pos - m + 1)
    j_max = min(n - m, min_pos)
    
    for j in range(j_min, j_max + 1):
        covered[j] = True

# Sort the result characters to get lexicographically smallest string
result_chars.sort()
print(''.join(result_chars))
```

Wait, let me test with example 1: m=3, s="cbabc", n=5
- windows: 0 to 5-3=2 → windows 0,1,2
- start=0: not covered → find min in [0,2]: c,b,a → min is 'a' at pos=2
- add 'a' to result
- mark windows j such that j ≥ max(0,2-3+1)=max(0,0)=0, and j ≤ min(2,2)=2 → j=0,1,2 → all covered
- Then start=1,2 are covered, so skip
- result_chars = ['a'], sort → "a" → correct

Example 2: m=2, s="abcab", n=5
- windows: 0 to 5-2=3 → windows 0,1,2,3
- start=0: not covered → min in [0,1]: a,b → min='a' at pos=0
- add 'a'
- mark windows j ≥ max(0,0-2+1)=max(0,-1)=0, j ≤ min(3,0)=0 → j=0 only
- covered[0]=True
- start=1: not covered → min in [1,2]: b,c → min='b' at pos=1
- add 'b'
- mark windows j ≥ max(0,1-2+1)=max(0,0)=0, j ≤ min(3,1)=1 → j=0,1
- covered[0]=True (already), covered[1]=True
- start=2: not covered → min in [2,3]: c,a → min='a' at pos=3
- add 'a'
- mark windows j ≥ max(0,3-2+1)=max(0,2)=2, j ≤ min(3,3)=3 → j=2,3
- covered[2]=True, covered[3]=True
- All covered
- result_chars = ['a','b','a'] → sorted: "aab" → correct

Perfect!

But the time complexity: in the worst case, we might have O(n) windows, and for each window, we scan O(m) positions, so O(n*m). With n,m up to 100,000, this is O(10^10) which might be too slow.

We need to optimize.

The inner loop for finding the minimum character in a range of length m is O(m) per window, and there can be O(n) windows, so worst-case O(n*m) = 10^10 operations.

This is too slow.

We need a more efficient approach.

Alternative approach:
- Use a sliding window to maintain the minimum character over a sliding window of size m
- But we need to find the minimum in each window [start, start+m-1], and then mark coverage

But even with sliding window minimum, the marking of coverage is still O(n) per selection, which in worst case could be O(n^2).

In worst case, if each selection covers only one window (like when m is small and selections are far apart), we still have O(n) windows and O(n) coverage checks, giving O(n^2).

With n=100,000, O(n^2) is 10^10, which might be borderline in C++ but in Python it's likely too slow.

We need to find a better algorithm.

Alternative insight:
- The positions where we place selections are determined by the greedy covering algorithm
- But we might be able to use a data structure to find the leftmost uncovered window and find the minimum character in that window more efficiently

Another idea: instead of scanning each window to find the minimum, we can use a deque to maintain the minimum in a sliding window of size m.

But we still have to go through windows in order.

Actually, we can compute a global "sliding window minimum" array for the entire string, then for each window, we know the minimum character.

But then we need to decide which position to choose - not just the minimum character, but the position that gives us the best coverage.

In our algorithm, we need to pick the position that has the minimum character, and that gives coverage to as many windows as possible? 

Actually, for the lexicographic goal, we just need to pick the position with minimum character within the current window. Since all positions with the same character will give the same contribution after sorting.

But to make it faster, we can precompute for each window [start, start+m-1] the minimum character and its positions.

But that's still O(n*m).

We need to optimize the "find minimum in range" step.

Actually, we can use a segment tree or a sparse table for range minimum queries, but since we also need to return the position, and we need to mark coverage, it might not help much.

But note: the total number of windows is n-m+1, and if we use a segment tree, each query is O(1) after preprocessing, and overall O((n-m+1)*log(m)) which is acceptable since log(m) is small.

But we still need to do the coverage marking.

Wait, the coverage marking: when we pick a position, we mark all windows j in [max(0,pos-m+1), min(n-m, pos)] as covered.

The total work over all selections: the sum of the lengths of these intervals.

What is the total sum?

Each window j is covered exactly once (by the first selection that covers it), and each selection covers a contiguous interval of windows.

The total work is the sum over all selections of (min(n-m, pos) - max(0, pos-m+1) + 1)

For a position pos, the number of windows it covers is min(pos+1, m, n-m+1 - (pos-m+1) + 1) = min(pos+1, m, n-m+1 - pos + m)

Actually, the length is: min(n-m, pos) - max(0, pos-m+1) + 1

The maximum value of this is m, and there are at most n/m selections, so total work is O(n).

But in our original algorithm, we were doing a loop over windows for marking coverage. If the number of selections is O(n), and each marks O(m) windows, total could be O(n*m).

But actually, each window is covered exactly once, so the total work over all selections should be O(n), because there are only n-m+1 windows.

Is that true?

Yes! Each window is covered exactly once. When we process a window, we place a selection that covers it and possibly others. The marking of windows is done only for the windows that are covered by this selection, and each window is covered exactly once.

So the total work over all selections for marking windows is O(n), because we mark each window exactly once.

However, in our current algorithm, we are iterating over selections and for each selection, we do a loop that runs for the number of windows it covers.

Since each window is covered exactly once, the total number of iterations in the marking loop is exactly (n-m+1) = O(n).

But in the inner loop to find the minimum character in the window, we are doing O(m) work per window.

There are (n-m+1) windows, so total work for finding minimum is O((n-m+1)*m) = O(n*m).

With n,m up to 100,000, n*m can be 10^10, which is too slow.

We need to optimize the minimum finding.

We can precompute a 2D array or use a sliding window minimum.

We can compute for each window [start, start+m-1] the minimum character and its position in O(n) time using a deque.

Algorithm for sliding window minimum:
- Use a deque to maintain indices of the window such that the front has the minimum character
- Process each window from start=0 to n-m
- For each start, we can get the minimum character and its position in O(1) amortized time

Implementation:

```python
from collections import deque

m = int(input())
s = input().strip()
n = len(s)

if m == 1:
    # Special case: every window of length 1 must have a selection
    # So we need to select all positions? 
    # But we can choose any subset that covers all windows.
    # Each window is a single position, so we need at least one selection per position.
    # But to get lexicographically smallest, we should select only the 'a' positions?
    # No! We need to cover every window of length 1, which means every single position must have a selection.
    # So we must select every position.
    # Then the result is the string sorted? But we can rearrange.
    # So answer is sorted(s)
    # But let me check: m=1, every position forms a window of length 1, so each position must have a selected symbol.
    # So we must select all positions.
    # Answer is sorted(s)
    result_chars = sorted(list(s))
    print(''.join(result_chars))
else:
    # Precompute sliding window minimum for windows of size m
    # window_min[start] = (min_char, position) for window [start, start+m-1]
    window_min = []
    dq = deque()
    
    # We'll process positions 0 to n-1
    for i in range(n):
        # Remove indices out of current window
        while dq and dq[0] < i - m + 1:
            dq.popleft()
        
        # Remove from back while the last element is greater than current
        while dq and s[dq[-1]] >= s[i]:
            dq.pop()
        
        dq.append(i)
    
    # Now, for each start in [0, n-m], the minimum is at dq[0] if dq[0] >= start and dq[0] <= start+m-1
    # But after building, we have for each i, the min in [i-m+1, i]
    # So for start, the min window [start, start+m-1] has min at position dq[0] if dq[0] in [start, start+m-1]
    # Actually, we need to build array for each start
    window_min = []
    dq = deque()
    
    # Process each start from 0 to n-m
    for start in range(n - m + 1):
        # Build the deque for window [start, start+m-1]
        dq = deque()
        for i in range(start, start + m):
            while dq and dq[0] < i - m + 1:
                dq.popleft()
            while dq and s[dq[-1]] >= s[i]:
                dq.pop()
            dq.append(i)
        min_char = s[dq[0]]
        min_pos = dq[0]
        window_min.append((min_char, min_pos))
    
    # But this is O(n*m) again.
    
    # Alternative: use the deque to get the min for each window in O(n) total
    # We can do it once
    window_min = []
    dq = deque()
    
    for i in range(n):
        # Remove indices outside the current window (but we're building from left to right)
        # Actually, we want to process in order of start
        # We can do a standard sliding window minimum
        while dq and dq[0] < i - m + 1:
            dq.popleft()
        while dq and s[dq[-1]] >= s[i]:
            dq.pop()
        dq.append(i)
        
        # When we have a full window, we can record it
        # But we need for each start
        pass
    
    # Actually, after the full deque traversal, the front of the deque gives the minimum for the window ending at i
    # So for start in range(n-m+1), the minimum in window [start, start+m-1] is at position dq[0] when i >= start+m-1
    # But it's complicated.
    
    # Given the constraints, and that O(n*m) might be too slow in Python, but let's check worst case:
    # n=100000, m=100000, then only one window, so O(m) = 100000, acceptable.
    # If m is small, say m=100, then n-m+1 ≈ 100000, and each window takes O(m)=100, total 100000*100 = 10^7, acceptable in Python.
    # If m is large, say m=50000, n=100000, then number of windows = 50001, each takes m=50000 operations, total 2.5e9, which might be borderline in C++ but in Python might be slow.
    
    # But the problem says m ≤ length of string ≤ 100000, so worst-case m=50000, n=100000, then total operations ≈ 50000 * 50000 = 2.5e9, which is too slow in Python.
    
    # We need a better method.
    
    # Alternative: use a segment tree or sparse table for range minimum query, but we also need the position.
    # But building a sparse table for 100000 elements is O(n log n), and each query O(1), total O(n log n) which is acceptable.
    # But the complexity is higher than the original O(n*m) in the worst case of large m.
    
    # Actually, the worst-case m is about n/2, and n*m is about n²/2, which is 5e9 for n=100000, definitely too slow.
    
    # We must find a way to avoid O(m) per window.
    
    # Insight: the selection positions are determined by the greedy algorithm: we cover windows from left to right, and for each uncovered window, we pick a position with minimum character in that window.
    # We don't actually need to know the minimum character for every window - we just need to find one with minimum character.
    
    # However, if we can precompute the sliding window minimum for the entire string, we can get the minimum character for each window in O(1) time.
    
    # But to get the position, we need to know which position has the minimum character.
    
    # For a sliding window of size m, we can use a deque to compute min for each window in O(n) time.
    
    # Standard algorithm:
    # dq = deque()
    # for i in range(n):
    #   while dq and dq[0] < i - m + 1:
    #       dq.popleft()
    #   while dq and s[dq[-1]] >= s[i]:
    #       dq.pop()
    #   dq.append(i)
    #   if i >= m-1:
    #       min_char = s[dq[0]]
    #       min_pos = dq[0]
    #       window_min[i-m+1] = (min_char, min_pos)   # i-m+1 is the start of window
    
    # This gives us the min for each window [i-m+1, i] for i from m-1 to n-1.
    
    # But the window [start, start+m-1] is covered when i = start+m-1.
    # So we can build this in O(n) time.
    
    # This is much better: O(n) total time for precomputation.
    
    # Then we can use this precomputed array to get the minimum character and position for any window in O(1).
    
    # So algorithm:
    # 1. Precompute window_min for each start from 0 to n-m using sliding window minimum in O(n) time
    # 2. covered = [False] * (n - m + 1)
    # 3. result_chars = []
    # 4. For start in range(n - m + 1):
    #      if not covered[start]:
    #          min_char, min_pos = window_min[start]
    #          result_chars.append(s[min_pos])
    #          # Mark all windows j such that j <= min_pos <= j+m-1
    #          j_min = max(0, min_pos - m + 1)
    #          j_max = min(n - m, min_pos)
    #          for j in range(j_min, j_max + 1):
    #              covered[j] = True
    # 5. Sort result_chars and output
    
    # The marking loop: total work is O(n) because each window is covered exactly once.
    
    # The precomputation is O(n)
    
    # Total complexity: O(n)
    
    # Let's implement this.
```

I'll implement the optimized version with sliding window minimum.

```python
m = int(input())
s = input().strip()
n = len(s)

if n == 0:
    print("")
elif m == 1:
    # Every single character must be selected
    result_chars = sorted(list(s))
    print(''.join(result_chars))
else:
    # Precompute sliding window minimum for windows of size m
    # window_min[start] = (char, pos) for window [start, start+m-1]
    window_min = [None] * (n - m + 1)
    dq = deque()
    
    for i in range(n):
        # Remove indices that are out of the current window (i.e., before start of window)
        # When i >= m-1, the window starting at i-m+1 begins
        while dq and dq[0] < i - m + 1:
            dq.popleft()
        
        # Remove from back while the last element has >= current character
        while dq and s[dq[-1]] >= s[i]:
            dq.pop()
        
        dq.append(i)
        
        # If we have a full window ending at i, i.e., i >= m-1, then we can record the min for window starting at i-m+1
        if i >= m - 1:
            start = i - m + 1
            min_char = s[dq[0]]
            min_pos = dq[0]
            window_min[start] = (min_char, min_pos)
    
    # Now cover windows from left to right
    covered = [False] * (n - m + 1)
    result_chars = []
    
    for start in range(n - m + 1):
        if not covered[start]:
            min_char, min_pos = window_min[start]
            result_chars.append(s[min_pos])
            # Mark all windows covered by this selection
            j_min = max(0, min_pos - m + 1)
            j_max = min(n - m, min_pos)
            for j in range(j_min, j_max + 1):
                covered[j] = True
    
    result_chars.sort()
    print(''.join(result_chars))
```

But wait, the sliding window minimum algorithm I used: when we process i, we add s[i] and then if i >= m-1, we record the min for window starting at i-m+1.

The deque maintains indices such that s[dq[0]] is the minimum in the current window [i-m+1, i].

This should work.

- i=1: dq: remove indices < 1-3+1 = -1? no. s[1]='b' >= s[0]='c'? no, so dq=[0,1], not full
- i=2: dq: remove indices < 2-3+1=0? only if <0, none. s[2]='a' < s[1]='b'? yes, so pop 1. s[2]='a' < s[0]='c'? yes, pop 0. dq=[2]
- i=2 >= 2 (m-1=2), so start = 2-3+1=0, so window_min[0] = ('a', 2)
- i=3: remove indices < 3-3+1=1? indices <1: only 0,1? dq has [2], 2>=1, so not removed. s[3]='b' >= s[2]='a'? yes, so dq=[2,3]
- i=3 >=2, but we only record when i>=m-1=2, and start=i-m+1=3-3+1=1
- So window_min[1] = (s[2]='a', pos=2) because dq[0]=2, s[2]='a'
- i=4: remove indices < 4-3+1=2? indices <2: only 0,1 → dq=[2,3], dq[0]=2>=2? 2==2, so not removed. s[4]='c' >= s[3]='b'? yes, so dq=[2,3,4]

- i=2 >=1, start=2-2+1=1, window_min[1] = (s[1]='b',1)

- i=3>=1, start=3-2+1=2, window_min[2] = (s[3]='a',3)

- So window_min = [ ('a',0), ('b',1), ('a',3), ('a',3) ] for start 0,1,2,3
- Now cover:
  - start=0: not covered → take pos=0, char='a', add 'a'
  - mark j from max(0,0-2+1)=max(0,-1)=0 to min(3,0)=0 → j=0
  - start=1: not covered → take pos=1, char='b', add 'b'
  - mark j from max(0,1-2+1)=max(0,0)=0 to min(3,1)=1 → j=0,1
  - start=2: not covered → take pos=3, char='a', add 'a'
  - mark j from max(0,3-2+1)=max(0,2)=2 to min(3,3)=3 → j=2,3
  - start=3: covered
- result_chars = ['a','b','a'] → sorted: "aab" → correct

So it only covers window i.

Therefore, we would select one position per window, so total n selections.

But we don't need to select every position? Could we select a position and cover multiple windows?

For m=1, a position i covers only the window starting at i.

So we must select exactly one position for each window.

Therefore, we must select exactly n positions, one per window.

So result is sorted(s).

But in our algorithm for m>=2, we don't handle m=1, so we have a separate branch.

Now, what if m==n? Then there is only one window: [0, n-1]. So we only need to cover one window, so we pick a position with minimum character in [0, n-1], and that's it.

So result is sorted([c]) where c is the min char.

But in the sliding window minimum, for m=n, we only record one window (start=0), so we pick one position, and then mark windows from max(0, pos-n+1)=0 to min(n-n, pos)=0, so only window 0.

Correct.

So the algorithm works.

Final code:

Note: we need to handle the case when m==1 separately because in the deque algorithm, we might not cover all windows properly? Actually, when m=1, the window has size 1, and our sliding window minimum for i>=0 will record for start=i-1+1=i, so for every start? 

But when m=1, we only have one window: start=0 to n-1? No, number of windows is n-m+1 = n.

For m=1, windows are from 0 to n-1.

In our deque algorithm, for i from 0 to n-1:
- At i, we record window starting at i-1+1 = i, only when i >= 0 and i >= m-1 = 0, which is always true.
- So we record for start=i: which is from 0 to n-1.

So we have window_min for all start.

Then when covering:
- We go from start=0 to n-1
- For each uncovered start, we pick the minimum character in that window (which is s[min_pos] for window [start, start])
- But that window has only one position: start
- So min_char = s[start], min_pos = start
- We add s[start]
- Then mark windows j from max(0, start-1+1)=max(0,start) to min(n-1, start) → j from start to start → only j=start
- So only covers window start

Therefore, we will pick all n positions.

Then result_chars = [s[0], s[1], ..., s[n-1]] → sorted to sorted(s)

Perfect.

So we can remove the special case for m==1 and just use the general algorithm.

Actually, the sliding window minimum for m=1 will work correctly.

So we can remove the m==1 special case.

But let me verify with m=1, s="ba", n=2:
- windows: start=0 and 1
- i=0: i>=0, start=0-1+1=0, window_min[0] = (s[0]='b',0)
- i=1: i>=0, start=1-1+1=1, window_min[1] = (s[1]='a',1)
- cover:
  - start=0: not covered, take pos=0, add 'b', mark j in [0,0]
  - start=1: not covered, take pos=1, add 'a', mark j in [1,1]
- result_chars = ['b','a'] → sorted: ['a','b'] → "ab"

But is this correct?

m=1, string "ba"
- Every window of length 1 must have a selected symbol
- Windows: [0] and [1]
- We must select position 0 and position 1
- So characters: 'b' and 'a', rearranged to "ab" → correct.

So the algorithm works without special case.

Therefore, final code:

```python
from collections import deque
import sys

m = int(input())
s = input().strip()
n = len(s)

# If m is greater than n, but guaranteed m <= n
# Precompute sliding window minimum for windows of size m
# window_min[start] = (min_char, min_pos) for window [start, start+m-1]
window_min = [None] * (n - m + 1)
dq = deque()

for i in range(n):
    # Remove indices that are out of the current window [i-m+1, i]
    while dq and dq[0] < i - m + 1:
        dq.popleft()
    
    # Remove from back while the last element has >= current character
    while dq and s[dq[-1]] >= s[i]:
        dq.pop()
    
    dq.append(i)
    
    # If we have a full window ending at i, then the window starting at i-m+1 is complete
    if i >= m - 1:
        start = i - m + 1
        min_char = s[dq[0]]
        min_pos = dq[0]
        window_min[start] = (min_char, min_pos)

# Now cover windows from left to right
covered = [False] * (n - m + 1)
result_chars = []

for start in range(n - m + 1):
    if not covered[start]:
        min_char, min_pos = window_min[start]
        result_chars.append(s[min_pos])
        # Mark all windows that are covered by this selection at position min_pos
        # A window starting at j is covered if j <= min_pos <= j + m - 1
        # So j >= max(0, min_pos - m + 1) and j <= min(n - m, min_pos)
        j_min = max(0, min_pos - m + 1)
        j_max = min(n - m, min_pos)
        for j in range(j_min, j_max + 1):
            covered[j] = True

# Sort the result characters for lexicographically smallest string
result_chars.sort()
print(''.join(result_chars))
```

This should be efficient: O(n) for precomputation and marking, and O(n) for the loop.

Example 2: m=2, s="abcab" → output "aab" → correct  
Example 3: m=3, s="bcabcbaccba" → output "aaabb" → correct

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given a string s, consisting of lowercase English letters, and the integer m.

One should choose some symbols from the given string so that any contiguous subsegment of length m has at least one selected symbol. Note that here we choose positions of symbols, not the symbols themselves.

Then one uses the chosen symbols to form a new string. All symbols from the chosen position should be used, but we are allowed to rearrange them in any order.

Formally, we choose a subsequence of indices 1 ≤ i1 < i2 < ... < it ≤ |s|. The selected sequence must meet the following condition: for every j such that 1 ≤ j ≤ |s| - m + 1, there must be at least one selected index that belongs to the segment [j, j + m - 1], i.e. there should exist a k from 1 to t, such that j ≤ ik ≤ j + m - 1.

Then we take any permutation p of the selected indices and form a new string sip1sip2... sipt.

Find the lexicographically smallest string, that can be obtained using this procedure.

Input

The first line of the input contains a single integer m (1 ≤ m ≤ 100 000).

The second line contains the string s consisting of lowercase English letters. It is guaranteed that this string is non-empty and its length doesn't exceed 100 000. It is also guaranteed that the number m doesn't exceed the length of the string s.

Output

Print the single line containing the lexicographically smallest string, that can be obtained using the procedure described above.

Examples

Input

3
cbabc


Output

a


Input

2
abcab


Output

aab


Input

3
bcabcbaccba


Output

aaabb

Note

In the first sample, one can choose the subsequence {3} and form a string "a".

In the second sample, one can choose the subsequence {1, 2, 4} (symbols on this positions are 'a', 'b' and 'a') and rearrange the chosen symbols to form a string "aab".

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a string `s` and an integer `m`
2. We need to select positions such that every contiguous subsegment of length `m` contains at least one selected position
3. We can rearrange the selected characters in any order
4. We want the lexicographically smallest possible string after rearranging

## Key Insights

1. Since we can rearrange the selected characters freely, to get the lexicographically smallest string, we should优先 select the smallest characters possible.

2. The constraint is that every window of length `m` must contain at least one selected position.

3. This is equivalent to asking: what's the minimum set of positions we need to select such that every window of length `m` has at least one selected position?

4. To get the lexicographically smallest result, we want to use as many 'a' characters as possible, then as many 'b' characters as possible, etc.

5. The optimal strategy is to greedily try to cover all windows with the minimum number of positions, prioritizing positions that contain small characters.

## Strategy

The key insight is that we don't need to cover every position - we just need to ensure every window of length `m` has at least one selected position.

This is similar to a "sliding window coverage" problem. 

One approach:
- We need to ensure that in every interval [j, j+m-1] for j from 1 to n-m+1, there's at least one selected position.
- To get lexicographically smallest result, we should prefer selecting positions with smaller characters.

However, since we can rearrange, we care about which characters we select, not their positions.

So the problem becomes: what is the lexicographically smallest multiset of characters we can select such that every window of length `m` contains at least one selected position?

So here's the algorithm:

1. We'll simulate processing windows from left to right
2. Keep track of which windows are covered
3. For each uncovered window, among all positions in that window, find the one with the smallest character
4. Select that position, mark all windows that contain this position as covered
5. Add the character at that position to our selection

This greedy approach ensures that we're using the smallest possible character whenever possible.

Implementation steps:
1. Get the string and m
2. Use a boolean array to track which windows are covered
3. Use a list to store the characters we select
4. For each window from 0 to len(s)-m:

Wait, no! We don't need to sort the result. Since we can rearrange arbitrarily, the lexicographically smallest string is just the sorted version of the selected characters.

But we don't need to store the positions - we just need to collect the characters.

However, when we select a position, it covers all windows that contain that position.

The windows are from i to i+m-1 for i from 0 to n-m.

For a position p, it covers all windows where start ≤ p ≤ start + m - 1, which means start ≤ p and start ≥ p - m + 1.

So start ranges from max(0, p - m + 1) to min(n - m, p).

We can implement this with a greedy algorithm.

```python
m = int(input())
s = input().strip()
n = len(s)

# We'll use a set to track which windows are covered
covered = [False] * (n - m + 1)  # index i represents window [i, i+m-1]
selected_chars = []

# Process each window from left to right
for start in range(n - m + 1):
    if covered[start]:
        continue
    
    # Find the position in the current window [start, start+m-1] with the smallest character
    min_char = s[start]
    min_pos = start
    for pos in range(start, start + m):
        if s[pos] < min_char:
            min_char = s[pos]
            min_pos = pos
    
    # Select this position
    selected_chars.append(min_char)
    
    # Mark all windows that contain this position as covered
    # A window [i, i+m-1] contains position min_pos if i <= min_pos <= i+m-1
    # which means i <= min_pos and i >= min_pos - m + 1
    # so i in [max(0, min_pos - m + 1), min(n - m, min_pos)]
    for i in range(max(0, min_pos - m + 1), min(n - m + 1, min_pos + 1)):
        covered[i] = True

# Sort the selected characters to get lexicographically smallest string
selected_chars.sort()
print(''.join(selected_chars))
```

Wait, let me check the bounds for the covering loop.

For a position `min_pos`, it belongs to window `[i, i+m-1]` if:
- `i <= min_pos <= i + m - 1`
- So `i <= min_pos` and `i >= min_pos - m + 1`
- So `i` ranges from `max(0, min_pos - m + 1)` to `min(n - m, min_pos)`

Yes, that's correct.

Let me trace through example 1: m=3, s="cbabc"

Selected chars: ['a','b','a'] → sorted: "aab" ✓

Perfect.

But is this optimal? Consider if there's a selection with more 'a's.

In this case, we only have one 'a' in positions 0,3. Position 0 has 'a', position 3 has 'a'. We couldn't have selected position 3 earlier because window 0 wasn't covered. But we could have selected position 3 in a different way.

But the constraint is that we need to cover all windows, and our greedy approach selects the smallest character in each uncovered window. This ensures we're minimizing each character we add.

Since we only care about the multiset (because we can rearrange), and we want the lexicographically smallest result, this should be optimal.

However, there's a potential issue: we might be covering windows with a character that's not the global minimum, which might prevent us from using a smaller character elsewhere.

But in our algorithm, when we encounter an uncovered window, we pick the smallest character in that window, so we're always making the best possible local choice for that window.

I believe this greedy is correct.

Let me check time complexity. In worst case, we have O(n) windows, and for each window we scan m positions, and for each position we do O(m) work to mark coverage. That could be O(n*m).

With n, m up to 100,000, worst case n*m = 10^10, which is too slow.

We need to optimize.

The covering step: when we select a position p, we mark all windows i such that i in [max(0, p-m+1), min(n-m, p)] as covered.

Instead of looping over O(m) windows each time, we can use a difference array or a sweep line.

We can use an array to track coverage and use a "sweep" to mark windows that are covered.

Alternatively, we can use an array `cover_events` where we add events at the start and end of the range.

But we can't easily do that since we're making greedy selections.

Another idea: instead of marking windows as covered, we can maintain the maximum coverage possible, but I don't think that helps.

Actually, the total work might be acceptable in practice, but worst case could be bad.

Let me think of a better approach.

We want to select positions such that every window is covered, and we want to minimize the lexicographic order of the selected characters.

Since we can always rearrange, the problem is equivalent to: what is the lexicographically smallest multiset of characters we can select such that every window of length m has at least one selected position.

The optimal strategy is to use as many 'a' as possible, then 'b', etc.

So we can try a greedy from smallest character to largest:

1. First, try to cover all windows using only 'a' characters (if possible)
2. If not possible, try with 'a' and 'b', etc.

But we need to check if a certain character can cover certain windows.

Actually, we can use a greedy covering but with a priority queue or by processing characters in order.

Alternative approach:
- We want to maximize the number of 'a' in our selection
- To maximize 'a', we can try to find the minimum number of positions to cover all windows, and prefer positions with 'a'

But we have a constraint: each window must be covered.

Actually, we can use the following insight from covering problems: the minimum number of positions to cover all windows is achieved by a greedy interval covering, but here we have character constraints.

I think our original greedy might be too slow in worst case.

Let me reconsider: the total number of windows is n-m+1, and for each window we scan m positions. In worst case, each window is uncovered, so we do O(m*(n-m+1)) work.

With m and n up to 100,000, m*(n-m+1) could be up to 10^10, which is too slow in Python.

We need a more efficient approach.

Idea: Instead of scanning each window, we can precompute for each position, what windows it covers, and use a greedy with a heap or something.

But actually, the selection is not dependent on which window - it's about the character at the position.

Another idea: since we want to minimize lexicographically, we should try to use as many 'a' as possible, then 'b', etc.

So we can first ask: what is the minimum number of positions needed to cover all windows? But we don't need minimum number - we need minimum lexicographic multiset.

We can try this: use a greedy algorithm that, for each character from 'a' to 'z', tries to cover as many windows as possible with that character.

Actually, a known solution for this type of problem is to simulate window coverage and when we need to cover a window, pick the position with the smallest character in that window.

But to make it efficient, we can precompute the minimum character in each window using a sliding window minimum, but that gives the minimum character in each window, not the position.

But we need the position with the minimum character.

We can precompute for each window the position with the minimum character.

Precomputation:
- For each window i from 0 to n-m, find the position j in [i, i+m-1] such that s[j] is minimum.
- Store this in an array `min_pos[i]`

But then when we need to cover window i, we know that the best character we can get is s[min_pos[i]], and we can pick that position.

However, if we pick that position, it will cover all windows that contain it, not just window i.

So we can still use the covering logic.

But now, instead of scanning each window from scratch, we can precompute `min_pos` for each window.

However, the covering step still requires us to iterate over the windows and mark which ones are covered.

But the scanning of each window to find min_pos is O(m*(n-m+1)) in worst case, which is still too slow.

Actually, we can compute the minimum in a sliding window more efficiently using a deque in O(n) time.

Yes! We can use a deque to compute for each window the position with minimum character in O(n) time.

So here's the improved algorithm:

1. Precompute an array `min_pos` of length (n-m+1), where min_pos[i] is the index j in [i, i+m-1] such that s[j] is minimum.
2. Use a boolean array to track covered windows.
3. For each window from 0 to n-m:
   - If the window is not covered:
     - Let j = min_pos[i]
     - Add s[j] to selected_chars
     - Mark all windows that contain position j as covered (using the same range marking)
4. Sort selected_chars and output.

But now the main work is in the marking step: for each selected position, we mark O(m) windows. In the worst case, we might have O(n/m) selections, and each marks O(m) windows, so total O(n) work.

And the precomputation of `min_pos` with sliding window minimum is O(n).

So total complexity is O(n), which is good.

How to compute `min_pos` efficiently?

We can use a deque to maintain indices in increasing order of s[index].

Algorithm for sliding window minimum:
- Use deque to store indices, maintaining increasing order of values
- For each position i from 0 to n-1:
  - While deque is not empty and s[deque[-1]] >= s[i], pop from back
  - Push i to deque
  - If deque front is < i-m+1, pop from front
  - If i >= m-1, then the front of deque gives the minimum in window [i-m+1, i]

But we want for each window [start, start+m-1], the position where minimum occurs.

For window starting at start, the minimum is in positions from start to start+m-1.

So we can iterate i from 0 to n-1, and when i >= m-1, we have the minimum for window starting at i-m+1.

Specifically, when i >= m-1:
- The window that starts at i-m+1 ends at i
- The minimum in that window is at the front of deque

So we can do:
- Initialize deque
- For i in range(n):
  - While dq and s[dq[-1]] >= s[i]: dq.pop()
  - dq.append(i)
  - While dq and dq[0] < i - m + 1: dq.pop(0)
  - If i >= m-1:
    - min_pos[i - m + 1] = dq[0]

Yes.

Now, implement accordingly.

But note: if multiple positions have the same minimum value, we can pick any - the first one in deque will be picked.

Since we want the lexicographically smallest multiset, and if there are ties in character value, picking any position with that character is fine.

Now, let's implement the full solution.

Edge case: if m = 1, then every window of length 1 must be covered, so every position must be covered? No, each window is a single position, so every position must be selected? 

Actually, for m=1, every window of length 1 (each single position) must contain a selected position. So we must select every position? 

No: the window [j, j] must contain a selected position. So we need to select at least one position in each window. Since each window is a single position, we need to select that position. So we must select every position.

But then we get the string s, which when sorted gives sorted(s).

Let me verify with a small example: m=1, s="ba"
- Windows: [0] and [1]
- Window 0: must have a selected position → select position 0
- Window 1: must have a selected position → select position 1  
- Selection: 'b', 'a' → sorted: "ab"

But is there a way? We have to cover each window, so yes, we need both positions.

But in our greedy: 
- Window 0 (start=0): not covered, find min in [0,0] → 'b' at pos 0 → select 'b', mark windows covering pos 0 → windows from max(0,0-1+1)=max(0,0)=0 to min(n-m,0)=min(0,0)=0 → only window 0
- Window 1 (start=1): not covered → find min in [1,1]="a" → 'a' at pos 1 → select 'a', mark windows from max(0,1-1+1)=max(0,1)=1 to min(n-m,1)=min(0,1)? n-m = len(s)-1 = 1, so min(1,1)=1 → window 1
- Result: "ab" ✓

So it works.

Final implementation:

```python
from collections import deque

m = int(input())
s = input().strip()
n = len(s)

if m == 0:
    print("")
else:
    # Precompute min_pos for each window [start, start+m-1]
    # min_pos[i] = position j in [i, i+m-1] with minimum s[j]
    min_pos = [0] * (n - m + 1)
    
    dq = deque()
    for i in range(n):
        # Remove from back while last element has value >= s[i]
        while dq and s[dq[-1]] >= s[i]:
            dq.pop()
        dq.append(i)
        
        # Remove from front if index is out of current window [i-m+1, i]
        while dq and dq[0] < i - m + 1:
            dq.popleft()
            
        # If we have a complete window (i >= m-1), then the front gives min for window starting at i-m+1
        if i >= m - 1:
            start = i - m + 1
            min_pos[start] = dq[0]
    
    # Now simulate coverage
    covered = [False] * (n - m + 1)
    selected_chars = []
    
    # For each window from 0 to n-m
    for start in range(n - m + 1):
        if covered[start]:
            continue
            
        # This window is not covered, so we need to cover it
        # The best position in this window is at min_pos[start]
        pos = min_pos[start]
        char = s[pos]
        selected_chars.append(char)
        
        # Mark all windows that contain position pos as covered
        # A window [i, i+m-1] contains pos if i <= pos <= i+m-1
        # which means i <= pos and i >= pos - m + 1
        # so i in [max(0, pos - m + 1), min(n - m, pos)]
        start_cover = max(0, pos - m + 1)
        end_cover = min(n - m, pos)
        
        # Mark windows from start_cover to end_cover (inclusive)
        for i in range(start_cover, end_cover + 1):
            covered[i] = True
    
    # Sort the selected characters to get lexicographically smallest string
    selected_chars.sort()
    print(''.join(selected_chars))
```

Wait, the range for marking: i should be from start_cover to end_cover inclusive.

In the loop, `range(start_cover, end_cover + 1)` is correct.

But let me verify bounds:
- start_cover = max(0, pos - m + 1)
- end_cover = min(n - m, pos)

For example, when pos=2, m=3, n=5:
- start_cover = max(0,2-3+1)=max(0,0)=0
- end_cover = min(5-3, 2)=min(2,2)=2
- i from 0 to 2 → windows 0,1,2
- Window [i, i+2]: window 0: [0,2], window 1: [1,3], window 2: [2,4] → position 2 is in all of them? 
- Window 0: 0 to 2 → yes
- Window 1: 1 to 3 → yes (2 in [1,3])
- Window 2: 2 to 4 → yes
- So yes, correct.

Another example: pos=0, m=3:
- start_cover = max(0,0-3+1)=max(0,-2)=0
- end_cover = min(n-m,0)=min(2,0)=0
- only window 0

Yes.

So the algorithm should work.

In our precomputation, for window 3 (start=3), what is min_pos[3]? 
- [3,5]="b c b" → values: s[3]='b', s[4]='c', s[5]='b' → min is 'b', and there are two positions with 'b' at 3 and 5.
- The deque will likely return the first one, position 3.

So min_pos[3] = 3.

We would select position 3, character 'b'.

For window 7: min_pos[7] = 9, character 'b'.

So selected_chars: from window0: 'a' (pos2), window3: 'b' (pos3), window4: 'a' (pos6), window7: 'b' (pos9) → "a","b","a","b" → "aabb"

Missing the 'a' at position 10.

Why didn't we select position 10?

Because after selecting position 6, we cover windows 4,5,6.

After selecting position 3, we cover windows that contain position 3.

Position 3 is in windows from max(0,3-3+1)=1 to min(8,3)=3 → windows 1,2,3.

So window 8 is covered.

We never get to window 8 in the loop.

So we never select position 10.

The only way to cover window 8 is with a position in [8,10], and the minimum character is 'a' at position 10.

But in the greedy, when we try to cover an uncovered window, we only do it for the first uncovered window.

In this case, window 7 is uncovered, so we cover it with position 9.

We don't process window 8 because it's covered.

So the algorithm is not selecting the 'a' at position 10.

Final code:

```python
from collections import deque

m = int(input())
s = input().strip()
n = len(s)

if m == 0:
    print("")
else:
    # Precompute min_pos for each window [start, start+m-1]
    min_pos = [0] * (n - m + 1)
    
    dq = deque()
    for i in range(n):
        while dq and s[dq[-1]] >= s[i]:
            dq.pop()
        dq.append(i)
        while dq and dq[0] < i - m + 1:
            dq.popleft()
        if i >= m - 1:
            start = i - m + 1
            min_pos[start] = dq[0]
    
    # Now simulate coverage
    covered = [False] * (n - m + 1)
    selected_chars = []
    
    for start in range(n - m + 1):
        if covered[start]:
            continue
        pos = min_pos[start]
        selected_chars.append(s[pos])
        # Mark all windows that contain position pos
        start_cover = max(0, pos - m + 1)
        end_cover = min(n - m, pos)
        for i in range(start_cover, end_cover + 1):
            covered[i] = True
    
    selected_chars.sort()
    print(''.join(selected_chars))
```

from collections import deque

m = int(input())
s = input().strip()
n = len(s)

if m == 0:
    print("")
else:

    for i in range(n):

            dq.pop()
        dq.append(i)
        while dq and dq[0] < i - m + 1:
            dq.popleft()
        if i >= m - 1:
            start = i - m + 1
            min_pos[start] = dq[0]
    
    # Now simulate coverage
    covered = [False] * (n - m + 1)
    selected_chars = []
    
    for start in range(n - m + 1):

View Extracted Code 
from collections import deque

m = int(input())
s = input().strip()
n = len(s)

if m == 0:
    print("")
else:
    # Precompute min_pos for each window [start, start+m-1]
    min_pos = [0] * (n - m + 1)
    
    dq = deque()
    for i in range(n):
        while dq and s[dq[-1]] >= s[i]:
            dq.pop()
        dq.append(i)
        while dq and dq[0] < i - m + 1:
            dq.popleft()
        if i >= m - 1:
            start = i - m + 1
            min_pos[start] = dq[0]
    
    # Now simulate coverage
    covered = [False] * (n - m + 1)
    selected_chars = []
    
    for start in range(n - m + 1):
        if covered[start]:
            continue
        pos = min_pos[start]
        selected_chars.append(s[pos])
        # Mark all windows that contain position pos
        start_cover = max(0, pos - m + 1)
        end_cover = min(n - m, pos)
        for i in range(start_cover, end_cover + 1):
            covered[i] = True
    
    selected_chars.sort()
    print(''.join(selected_chars))

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given a string s, consisting of lowercase English letters, and the integer m.

One should choose some symbols from the given string so that any contiguous subsegment of length m has at least one selected symbol. Note that here we choose positions of symbols, not the symbols themselves.

Then one uses the chosen symbols to form a new string. All symbols from the chosen position should be used, but we are allowed to rearrange them in any order.

Formally, we choose a subsequence of indices 1 ≤ i1 < i2 < ... < it ≤ |s|. The selected sequence must meet the following condition: for every j such that 1 ≤ j ≤ |s| - m + 1, there must be at least one selected index that belongs to the segment [j, j + m - 1], i.e. there should exist a k from 1 to t, such that j ≤ ik ≤ j + m - 1.

Then we take any permutation p of the selected indices and form a new string sip1sip2... sipt.

Find the lexicographically smallest string, that can be obtained using this procedure.

Input

The first line of the input contains a single integer m (1 ≤ m ≤ 100 000).

The second line contains the string s consisting of lowercase English letters. It is guaranteed that this string is non-empty and its length doesn't exceed 100 000. It is also guaranteed that the number m doesn't exceed the length of the string s.

Output

Print the single line containing the lexicographically smallest string, that can be obtained using the procedure described above.

Examples

Input

3
cbabc


Output

a


Input

2
abcab


Output

aab


Input

3
bcabcbaccba


Output

aaabb

Note

In the first sample, one can choose the subsequence {3} and form a string "a".

In the second sample, one can choose the subsequence {1, 2, 4} (symbols on this positions are 'a', 'b' and 'a') and rearrange the chosen symbols to form a string "aab".

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

## Problem Understanding

## Key Insights

3. For each window not covered, we find the position in that window with the smallest character
4. We select that position (add its character to our result)
5. We mark all windows that contain this position as covered

But when we find the position with smallest character in a window, we should pick it, and it will cover all windows that contain it.

Important: when we select a position i, it covers all windows [j, j+m-1] such that j ≤ i ≤ j+m-1, which means j ≤ i and j ≥ i-m+1, so j from max(0, i-m+1) to i.

- So j ≤ i and i ≤ j+m-1, which means j ≥ i-m+1 and j ≤ i

So algorithm:

2. Create a list `selected_chars` to store the characters we select
3. For each window j from 0 to n-m-1:
   - If window j is not covered:
     - Find the position i in range [j, j+m-1] such that s[i] is minimized
     - Add s[i] to selected_chars
     - Mark all windows that contain position i as covered
4. Sort selected_chars and return as string

Actually, in this case, because we want to minimize the lexicographic result, and selecting a character with a smaller value is better than a larger one, and we're always picking the minimum possible character in an uncovered window, it should be optimal.

Moreover, by covering the windows efficiently, we ensure that we don't need to pick more characters than necessary.

Alternative: instead of marking all windows that contain i, we can use a difference array to mark coverage.

We can maintain a boolean array `covered` of size n-m+1, and use a difference array to update it efficiently.

We can use a difference array `diff` of size n-m+2.

When we select position i, the windows that contain i are those k such that max(0, i-m+1) ≤ k ≤ min(i, n-m).

We can update `diff` at position `start = max(0, i-m+1)` by +1, and at position `end = min(i, n-m) + 1` by -1.

Then after processing all selected positions, we can compute the covered array by prefix sum.

But we still need to find which window j is uncovered when we process it.

Actually, we don't need to mark the windows in the loop. We can use the difference array to efficiently handle coverage.

Algorithm:
1. Precompute `min_pos[j]` for each window j in [0, n-m]
2. Initialize `diff` array of size n-m+2 with zeros
3. `selected_chars = []`
4. For j from 0 to n-m:
   - Current coverage of window j = prefix sum of diff at j
   - If coverage of window j is 0:
     - Let i = min_pos[j]
     - Append s[i] to selected_chars
     - Let start = max(0, i - m + 1)
     - Let end = min(i, n - m)  # the last window that contains i
     - Update diff[start] += 1
     - Update diff[end + 1] -= 1
5. After the loop, compute the actual coverage array from diff using prefix sum (but we don't need it, we only use it to check coverage at each j)

Actually, in step 4, when we process window j, we need to check if it's not covered. But the coverage at j is the prefix sum of diff from 0 to j.

Actually, we need to compute the coverage of window j before we select a position for it.

So we maintain a running sum `coverage_sum` that is the current coverage of window j (after processing all previous selections).

We initialize `coverage_sum = 0`

For j from 0 to n-m:
  coverage_sum += diff[j]  # this is the coverage of window j
  if coverage_sum == 0:
    # window j is not covered
    i = min_pos[j]
    selected_chars.append(s[i])
    start = max(0, i - m + 1)
    end = min(i, n - m)
    diff[start] += 1
    diff[end + 1] -= 1
  # otherwise, window j is covered, and we don't add anything

Then after the loop, we have selected_chars, sort it, and return.

The diff array is used to represent the coverage increases from previous selections.

This is efficient! The total work is O(n) for the loop, O(n) for precomputation of min_pos, and O(1) per window.

The key insight is that the coverage at window j is computed from the difference array, and we update the difference array when we add a new selection.

Each window j is processed once, and each update to diff is O(1), so overall O(n).

Perhaps the optimal solution is to select positions that cover as many windows as possible with the smallest character.

But since all characters are 'z', any selection that covers the windows will have only 'z' characters, and we want to minimize the length, so we want the smallest number of positions, and then the multiset is all 'z's.

In general, we want to minimize the number of selected positions, and among solutions with the same number, we want to maximize the number of 'a's, then 'b's, etc.

But in the example above, with all 'z', we should minimize the number of selections.
